| 1 | **Free Trade Agreement (FTA) between the EFTA States and the Republic of Serbia** | – Establishes a free‑trade area and aims to liberalise trade in goods, services, investment, government procurement, and intellectual‑property rights; <br>– Promotes sustainable development, environmental protection and fair competition; <br>– Reinforces democratic principles and human‑rights respect. | General objectives and preamble – [1], [2]; environmental and social references – [3]; trade‑facilitation provisions – [4]; procurement cooperation – [7]; investment & green‑economy clauses – [8] |

| 4 | **Obligations under the World Trade Organization (WTO) framework** (e.g., the Marrakesh Agreement) | The FTA expressly states that none of its provisions may be interpreted as exempting the parties from their WTO obligations, confirming that Serbia remains bound by WTO rules. | Preamble of the EFTA‑Serbia FTA – [1] |

### How these agreements fit together
1. **EFTA‑Serbia FTA** is the cornerstone multilateral trade deal that creates a free‑trade area between Serbia and the four EFTA members (Iceland, Liechtenstein, Norway, Switzerland).  
2. **Bilateral agricultural accords** complement the FTA by covering sector‑specific market‑access rules for farm products.  
3. **The Albania‑Serbia‑Montenegro FTA** shows Serbia’s participation in another regional free‑trade arrangement, extending its trade network in the Western Balkans.  
4. **WTO commitments** remain fully applicable, ensuring that Serbia’s trade policies under these agreements also comply with global trade rules.

If you need more detailed texts (full articles, tariff schedules, rules of origin, etc.) or information on additional Serbian trade pacts not covered in the excerpts, please let me know and I can help locate those documents.

### Q2: What is AI governance?

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 2.48s | 3.44s (+0.62s expansion) |
| Score avg | 4.8/5 | 4.0/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 7 |
| **Winner** | **✓** |  |

> **Judge:** Answer A covers an additional key aspect (corporate dominance) and cites sources for each point, making it more complete and specific than B.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

AI governance is the collection of policies, structures, and practices that guide how artificial‑intelligence systems are designed, deployed, and used.  In the material provided it is described in several complementary ways:

* **A shared, multi‑stakeholder responsibility** – it requires coordinated action among governments, industry, academia, civil society and the United Nations system [2].
* **A governance model that is currently dominated by a few corporations** – the existing multi‑stakeholder organization is noted to be “controlled by a few corporations” [3].
* **A shift from compliance‑only to a strategic, value‑creating function** – rather than being merely a defensive, risk‑mitigation activity, governance is framed as a proactive capability that can drive business success and innovation [5].
* **A focus on user control and security standards** – it involves designing AI agents so that people, in line with security requirements and personal preferences, retain the right to intervene when they do not want an autonomous decision to be made [8].

Taken together, AI governance is the coordinated, multi‑stakeholder effort to set and enforce the rules, standards, and values that ensure AI systems operate safely, ethically, and in ways that create strategic value while preserving user agency. [2][3][5][8]

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

AI governance is the set of structures, policies and practices that guide how artificial‑intelligence systems are designed, deployed and overseen.  In the discussions cited, it is described as:

* **A shared, multi‑stakeholder responsibility** – governance requires coordinated action among governments, industry, academia, civil society and the United Nations system [2].
* **A strategic, value‑creating function** – instead of being only a defensive, compliance‑focused activity, governance is framed as a proactive capability that can drive business success and innovation [5].
* **Human‑centric oversight** – governance means building AI agents so that, in line with security standards and user preferences, people retain the right to intervene when an autonomous system makes a decision [8].

Together, these points define AI governance as a collaborative, forward‑looking framework that ensures AI is developed responsibly, aligns with societal values, and remains under human control, while also being leveraged as a competitive advantage.

</details>

### Q3: What are india AI policies?

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 6.28s | 5.77s (+0.31s expansion) |
| Score avg | 5.0/5 | 4.5/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 3 |
| **Winner** | **✓** |  |

> **Judge:** Answer A is marginally more accurate and cites sources more comprehensively while matching the detail level of Answer B.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**India’s AI policy landscape (as of early 2024)**  

| Policy / Initiative | Core Elements | Status / Notes |
|----------------------|---------------|----------------|
| **National Strategy for Artificial Intelligence** (June 2018) | • Sets a “strong basis” for future AI regulation.<br>• Focuses on AI use‑cases in **healthcare, agriculture, education, smart cities & infrastructure, and smart mobility/transport**. | First comprehensive AI roadmap; still a guiding document rather than law. |
| **India AI Mission (2024)** | • **₹ 1.25 billion (≈ US $1.25 bn)** earmarked for AI.<br>• Investment in **computing‑infrastructure capacity, skilling, innovation, datasets, and safe‑trusted AI**.<br>• Includes free‑GPU access for researchers and funding for home‑grown AI models. | Launched March 2024; operational funding now flowing. |
| **Digital Personal Data Protection Act (DPDP) – 2023** | • Provides a legal framework for personal data protection, which underpins **responsible AI** (data‑quality, consent, accountability). | Enacted law; AI systems that process personal data must comply. |
| **AI‑related advisories (2024)** | • Guidance on **deep‑fakes** and **generative AI** use‑cases.<br>• Emphasises “safe and trusted AI”. | Non‑binding but widely circulated to industry and academia. |
| **IndiaAI portal (launched May 2022)** | • Centralised web portal for AI‑related developments, policy documents, funding calls, and best‑practice resources. | Public‑access information hub. |
| **Task‑Force Recommendations (2023‑24)** | • Create an **inter‑ministerial National AI Mission** to coordinate AI activities.<br>• Set up **digital data banks, marketplaces & exchanges** for cross‑industry data.<br>• Develop **operational standards** for AI‑based systems.<br>• Enact **enabling policies** (data‑ownership rules, tax incentives) to spur AI product development.<br>• Draft an **AI education & reskilling strategy** to build skilled talent.<br>• Participate actively in **international AI‑governance discussions** and bilateral AI partnerships. | Recommendations; many are being incorporated into the 2024 AI Mission. |
| **Principles for Responsible AI** (referenced in the Strategy) | • Emphasises transparency, fairness, accountability, and safety. | Not yet codified into binding law. |
| **Free GPU & Compute Support** | • Government‑provided **free GPU access** for research and model training; part of the “progressive” support mentioned in policy briefs. | Operational through public research institutions and cloud‑service partners. |
| **Regulatory Approach** | • **No binding AI legislation** yet; India follows a **flexible, principles‑based framework** that encourages responsible innovation while avoiding overly restrictive or state‑controlled models. | As of the writing, AI regulations do not carry the force of law. |

**Key Themes Across the Policies**

1. **Strategic Investment** – Large budget allocations (AI Mission) for infrastructure, datasets, and talent development.  
2. **Data Governance** – DPDP Act and task‑force calls for data‑banks/marketplaces, clear ownership and sharing rules.  
3. **Safety & Trust** – Advisories on deep‑fakes, generative AI, and “safe and trusted AI” principles.  
4. **Human‑Capital Development** – AI education, reskilling, and free compute resources to build a domestic talent pool.  
5. **International Engagement** – Explicit mandate to join global AI‑governance discussions and bilateral partnerships.  

Overall, India’s AI policy framework combines a **national strategy**, a **well‑funded mission**, **data‑protection law**, **principles‑based guidance**, and **institutional recommendations** to foster a sovereign, safe, and inclusive AI ecosystem while still awaiting formal, binding AI legislation.  

*Sources: [1] (National Strategy, AI Mission, DPDP Act, advisory notes, no binding regulations); [6] (Task‑Force recommendations); [7] (free GPUs & funding); [2] (infrastructure & policy support).*

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

India’s approach to artificial‑intelligence governance is a mix of strategic planning, large‑scale funding, and sector‑wide enable‑ment measures rather than a set of binding “AI laws.”  The main elements that have been articulated in the public record are:

| Policy component | What it entails | Source |
|------------------|----------------|--------|
| **National Strategy for Artificial Intelligence (June 2018)** | A high‑level roadmap that frames AI as a “scalable problem‑solver” for the country and identifies priority sectors – health‑care, agriculture, education, smart cities & infrastructure, and smart mobility/transport. | [1] |
| **India AI Mission (2024)** | A $1.25 billion programme that funds: <br>• Computing‑infrastructure capacity (e.g., free GPUs for researchers) <br>• Data‑set creation and “digital data banks, marketplaces and exchanges” <br>• Skilling, reskilling and AI‑education strategy <br>• Innovation grants for AI models <br>• Safe and trusted AI (advisories on deep‑fakes, generative AI) | [1] |
| **Free‑GPU & infrastructure support** | The government provides free graphics‑processing‑units and other compute resources to accelerate AI research and model development. | [2] , [7] |
| **Inter‑ministerial National AI Mission** | A task‑force recommendation to create a coordinating body that brings together all ministries for AI‑related activities, ensuring cross‑sector data sharing and standards. | [6] |
| **Data‑policy & marketplaces** | Enabling policies for data ownership, sharing rights, and usage; creation of “digital data banks, marketplaces and exchanges” to make cross‑industry data available for AI development. | [6] |
| **Tax incentives & other innovation enablers** | Fiscal measures (tax breaks, subsidies) to encourage private‑sector AI product development. | [6] |
| **AI education & workforce development** | A national AI education strategy to build skilled talent, plus reskilling programmes for the existing workforce. | [6] |
| **International engagement** | Participation in global AI‑governance discussions and bilateral partnerships for AI solutions and regulatory best‑practice sharing. | [6] |
| **Digital‑public‑infrastructure leverage** | The policy explicitly calls for building on India’s existing digital foundations (UPI, Aadhaar, digital identity) to deliver AI services (e.g., free AI‑driven doctors, tutors, agronomists) to citizens. | [3] , [4] |
| **Digital Personal Data Protection Act (2023)** | A privacy‑focused law that underpins AI work with rules on personal data handling, forming the legal backdrop for AI‑driven systems. | [1] |
| **Regulatory status** | As of the latest writing, India has **no binding AI regulations**; the framework is principles‑based and still evolving. | [1] |

**Key take‑aways**

* India’s AI policy is **strategic and investment‑heavy** – the $1.25 bn AI Mission, free compute resources, and tax incentives signal strong state support for AI research and deployment.  
* The **focus is on enabling ecosystems** (data banks, inter‑ministerial coordination, education) rather than prescribing detailed technical rules.  
* **Safety and trust** are addressed through advisory notices on deep‑fakes and generative AI, and through the broader Digital Personal Data Protection Act.  
* The government aims to **leverage its existing digital public infrastructure** (UPI, Aadhaar) to deliver AI‑powered public services while also positioning India as a global AI governance participant.  

Thus, India’s AI policy can be summed up as a progressive, resource‑driven strategy that combines a national roadmap, substantial funding, infrastructure provision, data‑sharing mechanisms, talent development, and a principle‑based regulatory outlook, without yet having enforceable AI‑specific legislation.

</details>

### Q4: what is AI diplomacy?

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |

| Time | 1.90s | 2.05s (+0.26s expansion) |

| Expanded docs | — | 6 |
| **Winner** | **✓** |  |

> **Judge:** Both answers are complete and accurate, but B provides slightly more concrete examples of AI tools, giving it higher specificity.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

AI diplomacy is the set of activities through which states (and other international actors) manage their relationships *about* artificial intelligence.  It encompasses three inter‑linked dimensions:

* **AI as a diplomatic topic** – the substantive issues that AI raises for international cooperation, security, ethics, standards and governance.  
* **AI as a diplomatic tool** – the use of AI‑driven technologies (e.g., data analytics, language‑processing, decision‑support systems) to augment traditional diplomatic work such as reporting, negotiation and strategic communication.  
* **AI as a factor reshaping the diplomatic environment** – the way AI changes geopolitical and geo‑economic dynamics, creating new opportunities and risks that diplomats must navigate.

In short, AI diplomacy is “how countries manage their international relationships regarding artificial intelligence,” covering both the policy questions AI creates and the ways AI itself is employed to conduct diplomacy [3][6][7].

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

AI diplomacy is the set of activities through which states (and other international actors) manage their relationships — and the broader geopolitical environment — around artificial intelligence.  It includes:

* **AI as a diplomatic topic** – the substantive issues that AI raises for international policy (e.g., standards, ethics, security, competition).  
* **AI as a diplomatic tool** – the use of AI‑enabled technologies to support traditional diplomatic work such as reporting, analysis, negotiation and strategic communication.  
* **AI as a factor that reshapes the diplomatic environment** – the way AI changes geopolitical and geo‑economic dynamics, creating new opportunities and risks that diplomats must navigate.

In short, AI diplomacy “refers to how countries manage their international relationships regarding artificial intelligence. It’s the whole range of activities nations use to deal with the challenges and opportunities presented by AI… a tool for diplomacy, a topic of diplomacy, and a force that is changing the geopolitical environment itself” [3][6][7].

</details>

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**State of the Internet in 2018 – Key Findings from the Available 2018‑focused Reports**

| Area | What the 2018 reports say | Source |
|------|---------------------------|--------|
| **Broadband penetration & mobile connectivity** | • The UN Broadband Commission’s *State of Broadband 2018* predicts that **active mobile‑broadband subscriptions will reach about 4.4 billion by the end of 2018**, a level that will “promote a mobile digital economy.”  <br>• The same report stresses that broadband is a catalyst for sustainable development and outlines actions for national leadership, demand‑stimulating measures, universal‑service reviews, digital‑skills training, support for local e‑businesses, adaptation of legal frameworks, and reduction of taxes/import duties on ICT equipment. | [4] |
| **IPv6 deployment** | • The Internet Society’s *State of IPv6 Deployment 2018* documents **steady progress in IPv6 adoption across countries, networks and service‑provider ecosystems**, indicating that the Internet’s underlying protocol stack is expanding to meet future address‑space needs. | [5] |
| **Affordability & economic impact** | • The 2018 Affordability Report (the fifth edition) notes a **growing body of evidence that higher Internet penetration is linked to stronger economic growth**, underscoring the importance of making connectivity affordable. | [7] |
| **Governance – a “tipping point”** | • Multiple briefing papers and analyses (e.g., “A Tipping Point for the Internet: Predictions for 2018” and “A tipping point for the Internet: 10 predictions for 2018”) describe **2018 as a pivotal year for Internet governance**, suggesting that policy debates, multistakeholder processes, and regulatory reforms are reaching a critical juncture. | [2], [6], [8] |
| **Overall snapshot** | • While the 2017 ITU‑UNESCO *State of Broadband* provides a baseline snapshot of global Internet access, the 2018 documents collectively show **rapid growth in mobile broadband, expanding IPv6 rollout, heightened focus on affordability, and intensified governance discussions**. | [1] (baseline) |

### Synthesis

- **Scale:** By the end of 2018, roughly **4.4 billion people** are expected to have active mobile‑broadband connections, representing a substantial share of the world’s population and a major driver of the “mobile digital economy.”
- **Technology evolution:** IPv6 deployment is moving forward, reducing the risk of address exhaustion and enabling new services.
- **Policy focus:** Governments and international bodies are urged to strengthen leadership, training, demand‑creation, universal‑service frameworks, digital‑skill development, and to lower fiscal barriers to ICT.
- **Economic relevance:** Greater Internet penetration is increasingly recognized as a catalyst for economic growth, making affordability a central policy objective.
- **Governance dynamics:** 2018 is widely characterized as a **tipping point** where the architecture of Internet governance—multistakeholder coordination, regulatory reforms, and global policy alignment—is being reshaped.

**Bottom line:** The 2018 reports portray an Internet that is **expanding rapidly in reach (especially via mobile), maturing technologically (IPv6), becoming ever more central to economic development, and undergoing a critical phase of governance evolution.**

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

| Area | What the 2018 reports say | Source |

| **Policy focus** | • The 2018 report highlights several policy levers for accelerating broadband: national leadership, demand‑stimulating training, review of universal‑service obligations, digital‑skills and literacy programmes, support for local e‑businesses, adaptation of legal frameworks to digitalisation, and reduction of taxes/import duties on ICT equipment and services. | 【4】 |

| **Governance “tipping point”** | • Multiple briefing papers and prediction pieces (e.g., *A Tipping Point for the Internet: Predictions for 2018* and *A Tipping Point for the Internet: 10 Predictions for 2018*) describe 2018 as a **critical juncture for Internet governance**, suggesting that debates over policy, multistakeholder models, and digital rights were intensifying. | 【2】【6】【8】 |

**Key take‑aways for 2018**

1. **Massive user growth** – ~4.4 billion mobile‑broadband subscriptions projected, underscoring the shift to mobile‑first connectivity.  
2. **Policy momentum** – Nations were urged to adopt coordinated broadband strategies, improve digital skills, and lower barriers to ICT adoption.  
3. **Technical evolution** – IPv6 deployment was advancing, addressing long‑term scalability of the Internet’s address space.  
4. **Governance focus** – 2018 was framed as a “tipping point,” with heightened attention on multistakeholder governance, digital rights, and regulatory frameworks.  
5. **Economic linkage** – Improved affordability was increasingly linked to broader economic growth, reinforcing the case for inclusive connectivity policies.  

These points together constitute the most comprehensive snapshot of the Internet’s state in 2018 that can be drawn from the supplied context.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**Answer**

The **Internet Governance Forum (IGF)** is a prime example of a multistakeholder platform that routinely convenes policymakers alongside private‑sector actors, civil‑society groups, technical communities and other stakeholders.

* The IGF’s Town‑Hall sessions (e.g., “Multistakeholder platform regulation and the Global South” at IGF 2023) explicitly bring together government representatives, private‑sector participants, civil‑society organisations, grassroots groups (women’s and youth organisations), and technical experts to discuss policy‑relevant issues and build consensus around shared goals and values【1†L1-L7】【3†L1-L9】.  
* High‑level sessions of the IGF further stress that the multistakeholder approach “encourages collaboration between governments, the private sector, civil society, and other stakeholders”【7†L1-L2】, underscoring the forum’s role as a convening space for policymakers.  
* The opening remarks of IGF sessions describe the platform as “a conduit for dialogue and collaboration among private entities, civil society, and government agencies”【8†L1-L2】, confirming that policymakers are a core constituency.

In addition, the **WSIS Regional Commissions** are also identified as multistakeholder platforms that bring together governments, the private sector and civil society, but the IGF is the most frequently cited venue in the provided material for convening policymakers in a structured, global‑level dialogue.  

**Key points**

| Platform | Role in convening policymakers |
|----------|---------------------------------|
| **Internet Governance Forum (IGF)** | Regularly assembles government officials (policymakers) with other stakeholder groups to discuss internet policy, regulation and governance. |
| WSIS Regional Commissions | Also multistakeholder, but the context highlights the IGF more prominently for policy‑focused convening. |

Thus, based on the supplied context, the IGF serves as the multistakeholder platform that convenes policymakers.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

Multistakeholder platforms are deliberately designed to bring **policymakers** together with a wide array of other actors—private‑sector firms, civil‑society groups, technical communities, youth and women’s organisations, and even grassroots networks.  The sources you provided repeatedly describe these platforms as **conduits for dialogue and collaboration** that enable governments to hear and respond to the needs and concerns of other stakeholders while also gaining timely insights for policy‑making.

* **IGF 2023 Town Hall #170** (the “Multistakeholder platform regulation and the Global South” session) stresses that the multistakeholder approach “allows for a broad range of actors to be considered in decision‑making, helping to build consensus around shared goals and values” and explicitly lists **government** alongside private‑sector, civil‑society and technical actors as participants【1†L1-L4】【3†L5-L8】.  
* **Main Session 3: Internet Governance and elections** notes that “this multi‑stakeholder approach helps in receiving timely insights and taking appropriate actions,” implying that policymakers benefit directly from the rapid feedback loop created by the platform【4†L1-L2】.  
* **High‑Level Leaders Session 3 (IGF 2023)** describes the approach as one that “encourages collaboration between governments, the private sector, civil society, and other stakeholders”【7†L1-L2】.  
* The **Opening of the session** explicitly calls the platform “a conduit for dialogue and collaboration among private entities, civil society, and government agencies”【8†L1-L2】.  
* In the **Networking Session #37 (Mapping the DPI stakeholders?)**, participants highlight that “multi‑stakeholder platforms create valuable opportunities for different actors to meet and engage,” which includes policymakers who might otherwise have limited access to certain civil‑society or technical groups【6†L1-L4】.

Together, these excerpts illustrate that multistakeholder platforms function as **convening spaces for policymakers**, enabling them to:

1. **Engage directly with diverse constituencies** (government, industry, NGOs, technical experts, grassroots groups).  
2. **Gather rapid, on‑the‑ground insights** that inform timely policy responses.  
3. **Co‑create solutions and build consensus** around shared goals and values.  
4. **Network informally** to foster understanding and future collaboration across sectors.

Thus, within the context you provided, a multistakeholder platform is precisely the mechanism used to convene policymakers alongside other stakeholders to shape inclusive, consensus‑driven internet and digital policy.

</details>

### Q11: what is serbia agreemnets

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|

| Score avg | 5.0/5 | 4.2/5 |

> **Judge:** Answer A covers both agreements plus an additional related framework, offering more thorough detail and citations, while both remain accurate.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**Serbia’s two principal international agreements that appear in the excerpts are**

| Agreement | Parties involved | Main purpose / scope | Key commitments that Serbia makes |
|-----------|----------------|----------------------|-----------------------------------|
| **Stabilisation and Association Agreement (SAA)** | Republic of Serbia ↔ European Union (EU) | A political‑economic framework that prepares Serbia for eventual EU membership. It links Serbia’s reforms to EU standards in the areas of democracy, rule of law, market economy, trade, environment, labour, and regional stability. | • Align Serbian legislation with EU law in the “relevant sectors” (e.g., oenological rules) [6]. <br>• Contribute to political, economic and institutional stability in Serbia and the wider region [6]. <br>• Gradually bring bilateral relations with the EU to the same level as EU‑Member‑State relations [7]. |
| **Free Trade Agreement (FTA) between the EFTA States and the Republic of Serbia** | Serbia ↔ EFTA members (Iceland, Liechtenstein, Norway, Switzerland) | Establishes a free‑trade area that liberalises trade in goods, services, investment, government procurement and intellectual‑property rights, while embedding sustainable‑development and labour‑rights objectives. | • Liberalise trade in goods in line with GATT 1994 [2]. <br>• Increase investment opportunities and develop a service‑trade environment [2]. <br>• Ensure fair competition and effective IP‑rights protection [2]. <br>• Gradually open government‑procurement markets [2]. <br>• Integrate sustainable‑development goals into the trade relationship [2, 5, 8]. <br>• Respect ILO obligations and work toward ratifying all fundamental ILO conventions [3]. |
| **Related policy framework** | Serbia ↔ EU (European Partnership) | The “European Partnership” identifies priority actions to help Serbia move closer to EU accession, complementing the SAA. | • Commitment to free‑market economy and sustainable development; EU readiness to support Serbia’s economic reforms [1]. |

### In short
- **The Stabilisation and Association Agreement** ties Serbia’s legal and institutional reforms to EU standards and aims at regional stability and eventual EU integration.  
- **The EFTA‑Serbia Free Trade Agreement** creates a free‑trade area, promotes investment, protects IP, opens public‑procurement markets, and embeds sustainable‑development and labour‑rights commitments.  

Both agreements require Serbia to progressively harmonise its legislation and policies with those of the EU or the EFTA states, while also contributing to broader goals such as sustainable development, market liberalisation, and regional stability.  

**Sources:** [1] (SAA preamble), [2] (FTA objectives), [3] (ILO obligations), [5] (sustainable‑development clause), [6] (legislative alignment), [7] (gradual alignment of bilateral relations), [8] (reaffirmation of sustainable‑development goal).

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**Serbia is party to two complementary international instruments that are reflected in the excerpts you provided:**

| Instrument | Main purpose | Key commitments that Serbia (and the other parties) have undertaken |
|------------|--------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Stabilisation and Association Agreement (SAA) with the European Union** | To bring Serbia’s political, economic and institutional framework into line with EU standards and to contribute to stability in the Western‑Balkans. | • Approximate Serbian legislation in the “relevant sectors” to EU law (e.g., oenological rules)【6】.<br>• Support the EU‑wide principles of the UN Charter, OSCE/Helsinki Final Act, Madrid & Vienna conferences, the Paris Charter and the Stability Pact for South‑Eastern Europe【1】.<br>• Commit to a free‑market economy, sustainable development and the “European Partnership” priorities that move Serbia closer to EU membership【1】.<br>• Align bilateral relations with the EU‑Serbia relationship as a model for future EU‑Member‑State ties【7】. |
| **Free Trade Agreement (FTA) between the EFTA States (Iceland, Liechtenstein, Norway, Switzerland) and Serbia** | To create a free‑trade area that deepens economic ties while respecting democratic values, human rights and sustainable development. | • Liberalise trade in goods in line with GATT 1994 Art. XXIV【2】.<br>• Expand investment opportunities and develop a services‑trade environment【2】.<br>• Ensure fair competition and robust protection of intellectual‑property rights【2】.<br>• Gradually open government‑procurement markets on a reciprocal basis【2】.<br>• Embed sustainable‑development objectives in the trade relationship and promote coherence between trade, environment and labour policies【5】.<br>• Honour ILO obligations, including ratifying fundamental ILO conventions and continuing efforts to adopt “up‑to‑date” conventions【3】.<br>• Recognise the agreements as the legal basis of a free‑trade area between the parties【4】. |

### In short
- **The SAA** is Serbia’s political‑economic pact with the EU, focused on legislative alignment, regional stability and the EU’s broader security and development frameworks.  
- **The EFTA‑Serbia FTA** is a trade‑focused pact that creates a free‑trade area, liberalises goods and services, protects competition and IP, opens public procurement, and ties all of this to sustainable development and labour standards.  

Both agreements together illustrate Serbia’s dual strategy of **(i) moving toward EU integration through legal and institutional convergence, and (ii) deepening market access and investment with the European Free Trade Association while up‑holding democratic, human‑rights and sustainability standards**.

### In short
Diplo advocates **an AI‑governance model that is (a) multistakeholder and bottom‑up, (b) firmly anchored in human‑rights, diversity and balanced data, and (c) guided by a philosophical, “human‑first” perspective that accepts human imperfection**. It couples this normative vision with concrete capacity‑building work (through the AI Lab and AI Campus) to ensure diplomats and other decision‑makers can operationalise these principles.  

All of these points are drawn directly from the supplied sources【1†L1-L2】【2†L1-L4】【3†L1-L4】【5†L1-L2】【6†L1-L4】【7†L1-L4】.

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

Diplo’s stance on AI governance is that it must be **rights‑based, multistakeholder and grounded in a broader philosophical view of technology rather than a narrow focus on data**.  

* **Human‑rights centred** – In the IGF 2025 session the foundation’s director, Jovan Kurbalija, stressed that AI systems should “uphold human rights, reflect diverse perspectives, and be built on balanced data” [2]. The same session linked AI governance to the broader human‑rights and privacy agenda that runs through Diplo’s work on internet policy [1].  

* **Global cooperation that respects local needs** – The IGF 2025 debate framed AI governance as “global cooperation meets local needs,” signalling Diplo’s belief that international rules must be adaptable to different cultural and regulatory contexts [1].  

* **Multistakeholder and private‑sector engagement** – Diplo calls for “enhanc[ing] the relevance and impact of multistakeholder forums…identifying incentives and formats that encourage broader and more active engagement” and explicitly discusses the role of the private sector in safeguarding AI‑related human‑rights [2].  

* **Philosophical and ethical framing** – Kurbalija’s “philosophical lens” questions the dominance of “data” in governance frameworks, pointing to a need for deeper ethical reflection on AI’s societal impact [1][7].  

* **Capacity‑building and diplomatic integration** – The Diplo AI Lab (established in 2018) provides research, analysis, and capacity development on AI policy, examining AI as a tool for diplomatic practice and as a subject of diplomatic negotiations [3].  

* **Balanced development over a “race” mentality** – Diplo’s 2019 briefing warned that the rapid proliferation of national AI strategies reflects a “race to become the global leader in artificial intelligence,” suggesting that Diplo favours coordinated, responsible development rather than competitive sprinting [6].  

Taken together, Diplo’s position can be summarised as: **AI governance should be a collaborative, multistakeholder process that places human rights, ethical reflection and local context at the core, while building diplomatic capacity and ensuring that private‑sector actors are engaged responsibly**. [1][2][3][4][6][7]

</details>

### Q17: digital diplomacy

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 2.10s | 2.67s (+0.21s expansion) |
| Score avg | 3.8/5 | 4.0/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 6 |
| **Winner** |  | **✓** |

> **Judge:** Both answers are equally complete and accurate, but Answer A provides more thorough source citations, giving it an edge.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**Digital diplomacy** is the practice of using digital technologies—most notably the Internet and social‑media platforms—to conduct diplomatic activities and advance foreign‑policy objectives. It encompasses a range of actions, such as:

* Communicating official messages and shaping a country’s image online.  
* Holding virtual meetings, negotiations, and multilateral consultations.  
* Creating digital platforms for public diplomacy, cultural exchange, and citizen engagement.  
* Addressing new agenda items that arise from the digital age (e.g., privacy, data protection, cybersecurity).  

In scholarly terms, digital diplomacy is described as “the use of digital technologies, especially the internet and social media, to conduct diplomatic activities and achieve foreign policy goals” [3]. It is also framed as the “use of the internet and social media for achieving diplomatic objectives” and as a shift in the environment in which diplomacy is practiced, bringing new topics onto diplomatic agendas [7].  

The concept overlaps with related terms such as **e‑diplomacy**, **web diplomacy**, and **cyber diplomacy**, but is distinguished by its focus on leveraging digital tools to complement traditional diplomatic methods and to manage the rapid, networked changes in demographics and technology [4][5].

In short, digital diplomacy is the modern, technology‑enabled extension of statecraft, allowing governments to communicate, negotiate, and influence both foreign audiences and other states through the digital sphere.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**Digital diplomacy** (also called e‑diplomacy or web diplomacy) is the practice of using digital technologies—principally the Internet and social‑media platforms—to conduct diplomatic activities and pursue foreign‑policy objectives. It encompasses a range of functions, such as:

* **Communication and outreach** – posting statements, engaging publics, and managing a country’s image on platforms like Twitter, Facebook, Instagram, etc. [3][7]  
* **Virtual negotiations and mediation** – holding meetings, negotiations and diplomatic consultations online. [4]  
* **Public‑diplomacy and cultural exchange** – creating digital platforms for cultural programmes, citizen‑to‑citizen dialogue, and multistakeholder engagement. [3][8]  
* **Agenda‑setting on emerging issues** – bringing topics such as privacy, data protection, cybersecurity, and internet governance onto the diplomatic agenda. [7][4]

The term is the most widely accepted label for the overall transformation of diplomacy in the Internet age, reflecting how the “networked shifts in demographics and technologies” complement traditional tools of foreign policy. [4]  

In short, digital diplomacy is the strategic use of online tools and social media to communicate, negotiate, and shape policy in a digitally connected world. [3][5]

</details>

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

The International Telecommunication Union’s core mandate is to **coordinate and safeguard the global use of the radio‑frequency spectrum and satellite orbits, improve communications infrastructure (especially in developing countries), and develop worldwide technical standards that make telecommunication networks, devices and services interoperable and universally accessible**【4†L1-L5】【5†L1-L2】【6†L1-L2】. In short, the ITU exists to “ensure networks and technologies” work together seamlessly, are affordable and universally available, and support sustainable social and economic development.

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

ITU’s core mandate is to coordinate and safeguard the global telecommunications ecosystem so that networks and technologies can inter‑connect, inter‑operate and be universally accessible.  As the United Nations‑specialised agency for ICTs, ITU is charged with:

* Coordinating the shared global use of the radio‑spectrum and satellite orbits, and developing worldwide technical standards that enable seamless inter‑connection of a wide range of communications systems [4]  
* Promoting, facilitating and fostering affordable and universal access to telecommunication/ICT networks, services and applications for socially, economically and environmentally sustainable development [1]  
* Facilitating global inter‑connection and interoperability of telecommunication facilities—devices, networks and services—through its standards‑setting and regulatory work, and by providing assistance to developing countries [5][6]  

In short, ITU’s mandate is to ensure that the world’s telecommunications networks and the underlying technologies work together reliably, are widely available, and support inclusive growth.   [4][1][5][6]

</details>

A **multistakeholder platform for dialogue** is an organized space that deliberately brings together the full spectrum of actors — government representatives, private‑sector companies, civil‑society groups, technical experts, and academia — so they can exchange views, negotiate policies, and co‑create solutions on shared digital‑policy challenges.  

The material you provided repeatedly points to this definition:

* **WS #97 “Interoperability of AI Governance”** describes the platform as “the most important, the multistakeholder platform… to have a dialogue with each other”【1】.  
* **WS #64** asks how to “foster more multilateral and more multi‑stakeholder dialogue” and notes that “there is no better platform for a true example of multi‑stakeholder interaction”【7】.  
* **Networking Session #37** and **IGF 2023 Open Forum #168** both highlight that such platforms “facilitate informal networking and dialogue” and “provide a platform for direct exchange between policymakers, regulators, businesses and civil society”【2】【3】.  
* The **IGF model** is repeatedly cited as a concrete example of a multistakeholder forum that “facilitates dialogue across different stakeholder groups”【4】【6】.  

In practice, a multistakeholder platform for dialogue typically:

| Feature | What it looks like in the cited context |
|---------|------------------------------------------|
| **Inclusive participation** | Government agencies, private entities, civil‑society organisations, and technical experts all sit at the same table (e.g., IGF, WS sessions)【3】【8】 |
| **Open, informal networking** | Sessions are designed to allow “informal networking and dialogue” that can lead to deeper understanding and collaboration【2】 |
| **Policy‑oriented exchange** | Topics range from AI governance interoperability to combating illegal content, cyber‑peace, and digital public infrastructure【1】【5】【7】 |
| **Facilitation of co‑creation** | The platform is used as a “conduit for dialogue and collaboration” to develop joint solutions rather than unilateral decisions【8】 |

Thus, a multistakeholder platform for dialogue is any structured, yet open, forum—such as the Internet Governance Forum (IGF) model or the various WS (working session) events referenced—that deliberately convenes diverse stakeholders to discuss, negotiate, and shape digital policy together.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

A **multistakeholder platform for dialogue** is a convening space that brings together governments, the private sector, civil‑society groups, technical experts and other interested parties so they can discuss, negotiate and co‑create policies or technical solutions.  

The examples in the provided material repeatedly point to the **Internet Governance Forum (IGF) model** as the archetype of such a platform:

* “the most important, the multistakeholder platform… you should use it as a platform to have a dialogue” (WS #97)【1】

Thus, within the IGF ecosystem, the **multistakeholder platform** is the structured, open‑to‑all forum (often organized as working groups, open‑ended sessions or “digital dialogues”) that enables **informal networking, formal discussion, and collaborative decision‑making** among all relevant stakeholders. It is deliberately designed to be inclusive, transparent and consensus‑oriented, making it the preferred venue for fostering multistakeholder dialogue on issues ranging from AI governance to cyber‑peace and illegal content.

Sorina Teleanu is a senior internet‑governance and digital‑policy expert who currently serves as **Director of Knowledge at the DiploFoundation**【6†L1-L2】【5†L1】.  

Her career spans more than 15 years in the field, including:

* **Governmental experience:** 2011‑2016 she was the alternate representative of the Romanian Government to ICANN’s Governmental Advisory Committee (GAC)【2†L1-L3】.  
* **Multistakeholder leadership:**  She has chaired the Executive Committee of the South‑Eastern European Dialogue on Internet Governance (SEEDIG) and has been a member of the UN Secretary‑General’s Multistakeholder Advisory Group for the Internet Governance Forum (IGF)【2†L4-L6】.  
* **Academic role:**  She is a visiting professor at the College of Europe, where she teaches a course on digital diplomacy【2†L7-L8】.  
* **Capacity‑building background:**  She is an alumna of DiploFoundation’s Internet Governance Capacity‑Building Programme, ICANN’s Fellowship Programme (now on the fellowship selection committee), the Internet Society’s Next Generation Leadership programme, and the European Summer School on Internet Governance【4†L1-L3】.  
* **Policy‑making experience:**  Earlier she worked as an advisor in the Romanian Parliament on ICT‑related legislation and policies【4†L4-L5】.  

In addition to these roles, she frequently moderates high‑level sessions on AI, internet governance and digital policy (e.g., IGF 2023, Policy Network on AI, IGF Parliamentary Track) and is the author of a book on the Global Digital Compact【1†L1-L3】【7†L1-L2】.  

Thus, Sorina Teleanu is a diplomat‑researcher‑educator who bridges digital technology, global governance and capacity development, holding both senior positions at DiploFoundation and extensive experience in multistakeholder internet‑governance bodies.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

Sorina Teleanu is a Romanian‑born digital‑policy expert and diplomat who now works as the **Director of Knowledge at the DiploFoundation**. She has a long track record in internet governance and multistakeholder diplomacy:  

* From 2011‑2016 she served as the **alternate representative of the Romanian Government to ICANN’s Governmental Advisory Committee (GAC)** [2].  
* She has held senior policy‑making and research roles in the Romanian Parliament (ICT‑related legislation), chaired the Executive Committee of the South‑Eastern European Dialogue on Internet Governance (SEEDIG), and sat on the UN Secretary‑General’s Multistakeholder Advisory Group for the Internet Governance Forum [2][4].  
* She is a frequent moderator and speaker at global fora – e.g., she moderated the **Main Session of the Policy Network on Artificial Intelligence at IGF 2024**, the **IGF Parliamentary Track**, and the **“Bottom‑up AI and the right to be humanly imperfect” session at IGF 2023** [1][6][7].  
* In academia she is a **visiting professor at the College of Europe**, teaching a course on digital diplomacy [2].  
* She is an alumna of several capacity‑building programmes (DiploFoundation’s Internet Governance Capacity Building Programme, ICANN’s Fellowship Programme, Internet Society’s Next Generation Leadership programme, European Summer School on Internet Governance) and now serves on the fellowship selection committee [4].  

Thus, Sorina Teleanu is a senior internet‑governance practitioner, educator and policy‑maker who bridges digital technology, multistakeholder governance and diplomatic practice. [2][4][5][6][7]

</details>

### Q24: what is digital diplomacy?

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|

| Score avg | 3.8/5 | 4.2/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 6 |

> **Judge:** Answer A is slightly more complete, mentioning cyber‑diplomacy and broader digital‑tool management, and it uses a broader set of citations, while both are equally accurate but lack concrete examples.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

Digital diplomacy is the practice of using digital technologies—principally the Internet, social‑media platforms, and other information‑communication tools—to carry out diplomatic activities and pursue foreign‑policy goals.  It reshapes the environment in which diplomacy is conducted, enables new forms of communication, public‑diplomacy, virtual negotiations, and cultural exchange, and brings issues such as privacy, data protection and cybersecurity onto the diplomatic agenda [2][3][4][5].  In short, it is “the use of the Internet and social media for achieving diplomatic objectives” and for managing the challenges of the digital age [5][7][8].

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

Digital diplomacy is the practice of using digital technologies—especially the Internet, social‑media platforms, and other information‑communication tools—to conduct diplomatic activities and pursue foreign‑policy objectives.  It reshapes the environment in which diplomacy is carried out, enabling states to communicate directly with foreign publics, hold virtual negotiations, create online platforms for cultural exchange, and address new agenda items such as privacy, data protection, and cybersecurity [2][3][4][5].  

In contemporary usage the term also covers the complementary role of diplomatic techniques in managing cyberspace‑related challenges (often called “cyber‑diplomacy”) and the broader effort to “manage change through digital tools and virtual collaborations” alongside traditional diplomatic tools [7].  In short, digital diplomacy is the “use of the Internet and social media to solve foreign‑policy problems and achieve diplomatic goals” [8].

</details>

### Q27: what is ping pong diplomacy?

| Expanded docs | — | 6 |

> **Judge:** Both answers are complete and accurate, but B provides clearer source citations, giving it an edge despite similar specificity.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

Ping‑pong diplomacy is the informal diplomatic initiative of the early 1970s in which the United States and the People’s Republic of China exchanged table‑tennis (ping‑pong) players.  The visits and friendly matches were taken as symbolic gestures of goodwill that helped break down ideological barriers, foster cultural exchange, and open the way for high‑level talks that eventually normalised U.S.–China relations【2†L1-L5】【4†L1-L4】.  It is widely cited as a classic example of “sports diplomacy,” showing how a seemingly minor sporting event can have a profound impact on international understanding and peace【1†L1-L4】【3†L1-L3】.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

Ping‑pong diplomacy is the practice of using sport—specifically the exchange of table‑tennis (ping‑pong) players—as a tool to open dialogue, build cultural understanding, and ease political tensions between rival states.  

- In the early 1970s the United States and the People’s Republic of China arranged a series of friendly matches between their national table‑tennis teams.  The visits were framed as informal, people‑to‑people contacts that symbolised goodwill and helped break the diplomatic deadlock that had existed since the 1949 Chinese Revolution【2】.  
- The “mistake” that sparked the first contact (a Chinese player’s unexpected visit to the U.S. team) was quickly seized on by both governments as an opportunity to signal a willingness to talk, and the sporting encounters paved the way for high‑level diplomatic talks that eventually led to the normalization of U.S.–China relations【1】.  
- Scholars describe the episode as a landmark case of **sports diplomacy**, showing how a seemingly minor cultural exchange can have outsized impact on international relations, fostering mutual understanding and cultural exchange while reshaping the geopolitical landscape【4】.  

In short, ping‑pong diplomacy is the historic 1970s U.S.–China table‑tennis exchange that demonstrated how sport can serve as a conduit for peace, cultural dialogue, and the thawing of hostile diplomatic ties.

</details>

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

The chapter begins by looking at how the United Nations itself first answered the wave of change that is reshaping global diplomacy. According to the text, the “initial reactions” are the **practical and policy‑oriented responses that emerged from within the UN system** – for example, statements, new procedures, and the mobilisation of agencies such as WHO, UNDP and OCHA, as well as the moral leadership of the Secretary‑General and the formal debates in the Security Council and the General Assembly [1][2].

At the same time, these internal responses are set against a **contest‑of‑multilateralism** that the authors describe as being driven by a resurgence of nationalism, populism and a growing preference for unilateral action by states and other actors. The chapter therefore frames the UN’s early actions as both a reaction to the challenges and a point of tension with the broader questioning of multilateralism itself [1][2].

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**Initial reactions from within the UN system and the contestation of multilateralism** refer to the first set of responses that UN bodies produced when the credibility of multilateralism began to be questioned.  

- **What the UN did:** The chapter notes that UN entities (e.g., the Security Council, the General Assembly, WHO, UNDP, OCHA, and the Secretary‑General) quickly issued policy statements, operational guidelines and diplomatic initiatives aimed at defending the multilateral agenda and demonstrating the organisation’s moral authority [2]. These “initial reactions” were meant to show that the UN could still coordinate collective action, even as criticism mounted.  

- **Why the reaction was contested:** At the same time, the authors point out that a wave of nationalism, populism and a growing preference for unilateral action were reshaping global politics. These trends “raise questions about the very idea of multilateralism” and present a formidable challenge to the UN’s traditional, collective‑decision‑making model [1]. In other words, the UN’s early responses were met with a broader contestation of multilateralism itself—states and other actors increasingly viewing the system as illegitimate, biased, or ineffective.  

Thus, the section “Initial reactions from within the UN system and the contestation of multilateralism” examines both the UN’s immediate policy and diplomatic moves to uphold multilateral cooperation **and** the parallel surge of criticism and unilateral tendencies that question whether multilateralism can survive in the current geopolitical climate.  

*Sources: [1] [2]*

</details>

### Q35: What are trade agreements solutions for WTO issues?

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 4.89s | 5.61s (+0.21s expansion) |
| Score avg | 5.0/5 | 4.2/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 6 |
| **Winner** | **✓** |  |

> **Judge:** Answer A covers a broader range of WTO challenges with concrete agreement names and mechanisms, and cites sources more thoroughly, making it more complete and specific.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**Trade‑agreement‑based ways to address the WTO’s current challenges**

| WTO issue / challenge | Trade‑agreement solution described in the sources | How it helps |
|------------------------|---------------------------------------------------|--------------|
| **Fragmented rules and lack of predictability** | Use of the core multilateral accords – Marrakesh Agreement, GATT, GATS, ITA/ITA II, TBT and TRIPS – that set consistent, transparent standards for goods, services, technology and intellectual‑property trade【5】. | Provides a single, predictable rule‑book that all members must follow, reducing legal uncertainty. |
| **Barriers to cross‑border digital commerce** | Negotiation of *digital trade agreements* that codify “secure and reliable frameworks for data exchange” and guarantee the free flow of data across borders【1†L1-L4】【2†L1-L4】. | Lowers technical and regulatory obstacles for online businesses, encouraging investment and innovation. |
| **Distrust in the digital economy** | Inclusion of trust‑building provisions (e.g., data‑security standards, privacy safeguards) within those digital agreements【1†L3-L4】. | Gives businesses confidence that cross‑border transactions will be protected, fostering greater digital trade. |
| **Unfair trading practices (IP theft, forced technology transfer, subsidies, state‑owned‑enterprise distortions)** | Creation of a *WTO‑reform working group* (U.S.–EU initiative) to redesign rules that curb such practices【6】. | Directly targets the “unfair” behaviours that undermine a level playing field, updating the multilateral system. |
| **Implementation gaps for the WTO Trade‑Facilitation Agreement (TFA)** | Use of *regional agreements* that tailor TFA measures to local circumstances and speed up implementation【7】. | Allows groups of countries to pilot and refine facilitation steps, feeding lessons back into the WTO framework. |
| **Dispute‑settlement delays and insecurity** | Strengthening the *WTO Dispute Settlement System* as a “cornerstone” that enhances security and predictability【8】. | Ensures that violations are resolved quickly and fairly, reinforcing confidence in the rules‑based system. |
| **Overall need for a more interconnected marketplace** | Broad negotiation of *new trade agreements* that link economies, reduce obstacles, and create a “level playing field” for all participants【3†L1-L3】【4†L1-L3】. | Encourages economic growth and development by removing trade frictions and harmonising standards. |

**In short:**  
The WTO’s principal “solutions” lie in expanding and updating its network of multilateral and regional trade agreements—especially digital‑trade and reform‑focused accords—that standardise rules, cut barriers, protect data and IP, address unfair practices, and reinforce the dispute‑settlement system. These agreements collectively aim to make the global trading system more predictable, trustworthy, and inclusive.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**Trade‑agreement‑based ways to address the WTO’s current challenges**

| What the solution does | How it works (as described in the WTO‑related sources) | Source |
|------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------|--------|
| **Creates a common rule‑book for digital trade** | Multilateral agreements (e.g., the TRIPS Agreement, the Information Technology Agreement, the Agreement on Technical Barriers to Trade) set consistent standards for intellectual‑property protection, data‑flow, and technical requirements.  By “establish[ing] consistent rules and standards, digital trade agreements reduce barriers for businesses operating across borders and foster trust in the digital economy” — they give firms a predictable legal environment for e‑commerce and cross‑border data exchange. | [1] & [5] |
| **Provides a forum for negotiating new or updated rules** | The WTO itself “provides a forum for negotiating agreements aimed at reducing obstacles to international trade and ensuring a level playing field for all, thus contributing to economic growth and development.” New negotiations can target emerging digital‑trade issues (e‑commerce, data localisation, AI, etc.). | [3] & [4] |
| **Uses the dispute‑settlement system to enforce commitments** | The WTO Dispute Settlement System is described as “a cornerstone of the multilateral trading system … designed to enhance security and predictability in international trade.” When a party breaches a digital‑trade rule, the system can compel compliance, reinforcing the credibility of the agreements. | [8] |
| **Sets up dedicated reform working groups** | In response to “unfair trading practices, including intellectual property theft, forced technology transfer, industrial subsidies, distortions created by state‑owned enterprises, and overcapacity,” the EU and the United States pledged to create a working group to address WTO reform.  Such groups can propose rule‑making or amendment processes that specifically target digital‑trade distortions. | [6] |
| **Leverages regional or “pioneering” trade‑facilitation agreements** | Regional agreements are recognised for “tackling specific trade challenges of member countries and facilitating the implementation of WTO Trade Facilitation Agreement measures at a regional level.”  They can act as test‑beds for digital‑trade provisions (e‑commerce rules, electronic customs procedures) that later feed into global WTO standards. | [7] |
| **Integrates existing WTO agreements into a digital‑trade architecture** | The core WTO accords – Marrakesh Agreement (establishing the WTO), GATT, GATS, ITA/ITA II, TBT, and TRIPS – together provide the legal scaffolding for digital trade.  By updating or interpreting these accords (e.g., extending GATS to cover online services, applying TBT to software standards), members can address gaps without creating entirely new treaties. | [5] |

### In short
Trade‑agreement solutions for the WTO’s current issues centre on **harmonising rules for the digital economy**, **using the WTO’s negotiation and dispute‑settlement mechanisms**, **creating specialised reform groups**, and **building on both multilateral and regional accords** to fill gaps in e‑commerce, data‑flow, and technology‑transfer rules. These approaches aim to lower barriers, increase predictability, and restore a level playing field for all members.

</details>

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**India’s AI politics — a strategic, cross‑sectoral policy agenda**

| Dimension | Core political stance | How it is being operationalised | Key references |
|-----------|----------------------|--------------------------------|----------------|
| **National‑interest framing** | AI is treated as a pillar of India’s economic productivity, military power and information control. | The government is positioning AI as essential to growth, defence and the ability to shape public discourse. | [1], [2], [4], [8] |
| **Universal public‑service model** | The first wave of AI deployment is to be free, citizen‑centric services (e‑health, tutoring, agronomy) delivered through the Aadhaar identity stack. | Pilot programmes will embed AI assistants in existing public‑service platforms before any commercial‑grade, “disruptive” applications are released. | [1], [2] |
| **Leveraging digital public infrastructure** | India wants to export its “digital public‑goods” play‑book (UPI, digital ID) to AI governance, using the same trust‑by‑design principles that made those systems successful. | Policy discussions stress that AI governance should be built on the same open, interoperable, and secure foundations that underpin UPI and Aadhaar. | [3], [6] |
| **Balanced regulatory posture** | The aim is to avoid the extremes of: <br>• Over‑regulation à la Europe, <br>• Laissez‑faire market‑only models of the United States, <br>• State‑centric, surveillance‑heavy models of China. | A “risk‑based” AI policy is being drafted for sectors such as finance, while sector‑specific ethical councils will issue privacy, ethics and security guidelines. | [3], [5], [6] |
| **State‑backed AI ecosystem** | The government will directly fund and supply the technical stack needed for AI research and deployment. | Initiatives include free GPU allocations, dedicated funding for large‑scale models, and investment in energy‑grid capacity to support compute‑intensive workloads. | [7] |
| **Ethical and sectoral governance** | Creation of domain‑specific ethical councils to craft sector‑level standards on privacy, ethics and security. | These councils will work alongside the broader national AI strategy to ensure responsible adoption across health, education, agriculture, defence, etc. | [5] |

### Summary

India’s AI politics is a **strategic, state‑driven approach** that treats artificial intelligence as a national security and development asset. The policy agenda:

1. **Prioritises public‑good AI services** (free doctors, tutors, agronomists) delivered through the Aadhaar identity platform.  
2. **Builds on India’s proven digital‑public‑infrastructure (UPI, digital ID)** to create a trusted, interoperable AI ecosystem.  
3. **Adopts a “middle‑path” regulatory model**, steering clear of overly restrictive European rules, the hands‑off US model, or the state‑controlled Chinese paradigm.  
4. **Institutes sector‑specific ethical councils** to guide privacy, ethics and security standards.  
5. **Provides direct state support** (free GPUs, funding, energy infrastructure) to accelerate AI research and deployment.

Together, these elements constitute what the sources describe as **India’s AI politics**—a coordinated, sovereign‑focused, and ethically‑anchored policy framework aimed at harnessing AI for economic growth, defence capability, and information governance.  

**Sources:** [1]–[8] (see context entries for each point).

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

India’s AI politics is framed as a **strategic, state‑driven agenda that ties artificial‑intelligence development to the country’s core national interests** – economic growth, defence capability and information control.

* **Economic productivity** – The government sees AI as a catalyst for higher output across sectors and is already committing public resources such as free GPUs, funding for large‑scale models and energy‑infrastructure support to spur domestic AI innovation【7】.  

* **Military power** – AI is positioned as essential for modernising India’s defence forces and maintaining strategic parity, a view repeatedly stressed in the Invest India fireside chats【1】【2】【4】【8】.  

* **Information control** – By embedding AI services (e‑health, tutoring, agronomy) into the Aadhaar identity stack, the state aims to deliver “free AI services” to citizens while simultaneously creating a data‑rich platform that can be leveraged for broader information‑management objectives【1】【2】.  

* **Governance model** – India wants to chart a middle path in global AI governance: it will draw on its successful digital‑public‑infrastructure experience (UPI, digital identity) but will **avoid the overly restrictive European regime, the laissez‑faire American approach, and the state‑centric Chinese model**【3】【6】.  

* **Regulatory architecture** – The National AI Strategy calls for **domain‑specific ethical councils** that will draft sector‑wise guidelines on privacy, ethics and security, ensuring that AI deployment aligns with national policy goals while addressing societal concerns【5】.  

In sum, India’s AI politics is a **co‑ordinated policy blend of state‑led service provision, strategic defence use, and a calibrated regulatory stance that leverages existing digital infrastructure to shape both domestic and international AI governance**【1】【3】【5】【7】.

</details>

> **Judge:** Answer A covers all key pillars (including sustainability), offers concrete actions and metrics, and references sources more thoroughly than B.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**Building an Inclusive and Resilient Digital Future – What the WAIGF 2024 Tells Us**

| Core Pillar | What the WAIGF says it means | Why it matters | How to put it into practice |
|-------------|-----------------------------|----------------|-----------------------------|
| **Inclusivity** | “A digital future that is *inclusive, human‑centric and equitable*” (Keynotes [2]; Wrap‑up [4]) | Guarantees that all people—regardless of gender, age, ability, language or geography—can benefit from digital services. | • Launch **local and national initiatives** that target under‑served groups (Open Forum #12 [6]). <br>• Invest in **capacity‑building** for diverse stakeholders so they can meaningfully participate (Open Forum #12 [7]). <br>• Adopt **rights‑respecting policies** (e.g., accessibility standards, language localisation, data‑protection safeguards). |
| **Resilience** | “A digital future that is *resilient* and *safe*” (Opening Ceremony [1]; High‑Level Youth IGF [3]) | Enables societies to withstand shocks—cyber‑attacks, natural disasters, or sudden policy changes—while keeping essential services online. | • Create **multi‑sector coordination mechanisms** (government, private sector, civil society) for rapid response (High‑Level Youth IGF [5]). <br>• Develop **redundant infrastructure** (e.g., mesh networks, backup data centres). <br>• Implement **continuous risk‑assessment** and incident‑response drills. |
| **Safety & Rights‑Respect** | “Ensuring an *inclusive and rights‑respecting* digital future” (Open Forum #12 [6]) | Protects users from abuse, discrimination, and privacy violations, fostering trust and adoption. | • Enforce **privacy‑by‑design** and **data‑minimisation** standards. <br>• Adopt **online‑harassment and hate‑speech mitigation** tools. <br>• Provide **transparent grievance mechanisms** for users. |
| **Collaboration** | “Shared responsibility requiring coordinated efforts across sectors and stakeholders” (High‑Level Youth IGF [5]) | No single actor can deliver the whole ecosystem; synergy multiplies impact. | • Set up **multi‑stakeholder advisory boards** at national and regional levels. <br>• Facilitate **public‑private partnerships** for infrastructure financing and skill‑transfer. <br>• Engage **youth and community groups** early to co‑design solutions. |
| **Sustainability** | “Inclusive, equitable and *sustainable*” (Keynotes [2]) | Ensures that digital progress does not deplete resources or exacerbate inequality over time. | • Promote **energy‑efficient technologies** (e.g., low‑power IoT, renewable‑powered data centres). <br>• Adopt **circular‑economy practices** for e‑waste. <br>• Measure impact with **social‑impact indicators** (digital literacy rates, gender parity, rural connectivity). |

---

### A Practical Road‑Map for West Africa (and beyond)

1. **Stakeholder Mapping & Governance**  
   *Identify* government ministries, telecom operators, fintech firms, NGOs, academia, and youth networks.  
   *Create* a **High‑Level Digital Futures Council** that meets quarterly (mirroring the WAIGF high‑level session [5]).

2. **Policy Foundations**  
   - Draft a **National Inclusive Digital Strategy** that embeds human‑centred design, data‑rights, and resilience clauses.  
   - Align with regional frameworks (e.g., African Union’s Digital Transformation Strategy) to ensure coherence.

3. **Capacity‑Building Programs**  
   - Launch **Digital Literacy Hubs** in rural schools and community centres (Open Forum #12 [7]).  
   - Offer **certified training** for women, persons with disabilities, and youth in cybersecurity, data analytics, and entrepreneurship.

4. **Infrastructure & Safety Layers**  
   - Deploy **community mesh networks** to provide backup connectivity during outages.  
   - Establish a **National Cyber‑Resilience Centre** for threat monitoring and rapid incident response (Resilience [1]).

5. **Rights‑Respecting Services**  
   - Mandate **accessibility standards** for all public digital services.  
   - Implement **privacy impact assessments** before launching new platforms.

6. **Monitoring, Evaluation & Adaptation**  
   - Use a **dashboard of inclusive‑resilience indicators** (e.g., % of population with affordable broadband, number of cyber‑incidents mitigated, gender parity in digital jobs).  
   - Conduct **annual multi‑stakeholder reviews** to adjust policies and funding.

---

### Key Take‑aways from the WAIGF

- **Inclusivity and resilience are not optional add‑ons**; they are core design principles repeatedly emphasized across the event (Opening Ceremony [1]; Keynotes [2]; Wrap‑up [4]).  
- **Collaboration is the engine**—government, private sector, and citizens must act together (High‑Level Youth IGF [3]; discussion underscored shared responsibility [5]).  
- **Capacity building and rights‑respect are the practical levers** that turn high‑level rhetoric into everyday reality (Open Forum #12 [6] & [7]).

By embedding these pillars into policy, investment, and community action, West Africa—and any region—can move from vision to a **tangible, inclusive, and resilient digital future**.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**What a “inclusive and resilient digital future” looks like – and how to get there**

| Core Idea | What the WAIGF 2024 material says | Practical take‑aways |
|-----------|-----------------------------------|----------------------|
| **Human‑centric, inclusive design** | “Let us build a digital future that’s inclusive, human‑centric and resilient” (Keynotes [2]; Wrap‑up [4]) | • Put people – especially marginalized groups, youth, women, persons with disabilities – at the centre of policy and product design.<br>• Conduct participatory assessments to identify barriers (e.g., language, connectivity, digital literacy). |
| **Safety & rights‑respect** | “Building a safe and inclusive digital future is a shared responsibility” (High‑Level Youth IGF [5]) | • Embed privacy‑by‑design, data‑protection, and online‑harassment safeguards in all platforms.<br>• Adopt rights‑based frameworks that guarantee freedom of expression and non‑discrimination. |
| **Resilience (technical & societal)** | “Building a resilient, inclusive and safe digital future” (Opening Ceremony [1]; multiple headings [3], [5], [8]) | • Strengthen infrastructure (e.g., redundant networks, local data centres) to withstand shocks.<br>• Build institutional capacity to respond to cyber‑threats, misinformation, and service outages. |
| **Multi‑stakeholder collaboration** | “Collaboration between government, private sector, and citizens” (High‑Level Youth IGF [3])<br>“Coordinated efforts across sectors and stakeholders” (High‑Level Youth IGF [5]) | • Create formal multi‑stakeholder forums (government, industry, civil society, academia, youth) with clear mandates.<br>• Draft joint action plans that allocate responsibilities, resources, and timelines. |
| **Capacity‑building for diverse actors** | “Building capacity and expertise among diverse stakeholders to enable effective participation” (Open Forum #12 [7]) | • Offer training programmes, mentorship, and toolkits for policymakers, journalists, community leaders, and entrepreneurs.<br>• Prioritise local languages and context‑specific curricula. |
| **Local & national initiatives as pilots** | “What does this look like in practice? Local and National Initiatives” (Open Forum #12 [6]) | • Scale successful community‑driven projects (e.g., community Wi‑Fi, digital literacy clubs) to national policy.<br>• Use pilot results to inform regulations, standards, and funding mechanisms. |

### Step‑by‑step roadmap (derived from the themes above)

1. **Define shared goals** – Agree on a common vision: an inclusive, human‑centric, safe, and resilient digital ecosystem (as repeatedly stated in the opening and wrap‑up remarks [1], [2], [4]).
2. **Map stakeholders & responsibilities** – Identify government agencies, private‑sector players, civil‑society groups, and youth networks; assign clear roles (collaboration call‑out [3], [5]).
3. **Conduct a baseline assessment** – Measure current gaps in access, safety, and resilience; include community‑level data (the “local and national initiatives” lens [6]).
4. **Develop a rights‑based policy framework** – Enshrine privacy, non‑discrimination, and freedom of expression; embed safety standards (rights‑respecting focus [6]).
5. **Invest in infrastructure resilience** – Deploy redundant networks, promote local data‑hosting, and plan for disaster recovery (resilience emphasis [1], [8]).
6. **Launch capacity‑building programmes** – Train diverse stakeholders to participate meaningfully (capacity‑building note [7]).
7. **Pilot and scale local solutions** – Test community Wi‑Fi, digital‑literacy hubs, or youth‑led cyber‑security labs; use lessons to inform national policy (local initiatives [6]).
8. **Monitor, evaluate, and iterate** – Set measurable indicators (inclusion index, outage frequency, incident response time) and review progress regularly in multi‑stakeholder forums.

### Key take‑aways for policymakers and practitioners

- **Inclusivity is not optional** – It must be baked into every stage, from design to implementation (Keynotes [2]; Open Forum [7]).
- **Resilience requires both tech and governance** – Robust infrastructure must be matched by strong institutions and coordinated response mechanisms (Opening Ceremony [1]; High‑Level WAIGF [8]).
- **Shared responsibility** – No single actor can deliver the vision alone; coordinated, cross‑sector effort is repeatedly highlighted (High‑Level Youth IGF [3], [5]).
- **Rights‑respecting practice** – Safety and human rights go hand‑in‑hand; policies should protect users while enabling participation (Open Forum #12 [6]).

By following this multi‑dimensional, collaborative roadmap—grounded in the WAIGF 2024 messages—you can move from rhetoric to concrete actions that build an **inclusive, resilient, and safe digital future** for West Africa and beyond.

</details>

During the first wave of the coronavirus pandemic Norway was already “well‑positioned to meet the crisis,” and the shock of the lockdown in March 2020 turned the health emergency into a powerful catalyst for digitalisation across the whole economy [1].  

Key points from the report *Our new digital world: Digitalisation in Norway during the coronavirus pandemic* are:

| Area | What the pandemic triggered |
|------|-----------------------------|
| **Public sector** | A “pioneering public sector” that accelerated the rollout of digital services and e‑government tools [1]. |
| **Industry** | Rapid digital transformation in **aquaculture**, **maritime**, **agriculture** and **tourism**, each receiving dedicated chapters that describe how new data platforms, remote monitoring and online booking systems were introduced to keep businesses operating while physical contact was limited [1]. |
| **Overall pace** | The pandemic “accelerated the digitalisation of society and the business sector” in a number of areas, confirming that the lockdown acted as a catalyst for change [2]. |
| **Strategic outlook** | The report ends with a “road ahead” section that outlines how the momentum will be turned into long‑term structural reforms [1]. |

The Norwegian government framed this surge as part of a broader vision: Minister of Digitalisation and Public Governance **Karianne Tung** repeatedly stressed that “digitalisation is really the tool we need to solve huge societal challenges” and that the country aims to make digitalisation work “for people” while managing it responsibly, including the use of artificial intelligence [6].

In short, COVID‑19 did not just force temporary remote work in Norway; it unlocked a nationwide push to embed digital technologies in public services and key economic sectors, positioning the country to continue expanding its digital infrastructure long after the pandemic subsides. [1][2][6]

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

During the first wave of the pandemic Norway’s digital transformation really took off. The lockdown that began in March 2020 acted as a “catalyst for digitalisation” – the country’s existing digital infrastructure and public‑sector expertise meant it was already well‑positioned to cope with the crisis and then to accelerate the rollout of digital services across the economy [1].  

In the months that followed, both society at large and the business sector saw a rapid increase in the use of digital tools and platforms, as organisations rushed to replace face‑to‑face interactions with online alternatives.  This surge in adoption was noted as a key feature of the post‑COVID landscape, with digitalisation “accelerated in a number of areas” after the March 2020 lockdown [2].  

In short, the pandemic did not just force temporary remote work; it sparked a broader, lasting push to embed digital technologies throughout Norwegian public services, industry, and everyday life.

</details>
