<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

India’s AI roadmap is being built around **sovereignty, scale‑efficient innovation, and public‑service orientation**.

| Pillar | What it means | Key points from the discussion |
|--------|----------------|---------------------------------|
| **Energy & Compute Sovereignty** | Build a home‑grown, low‑cost compute fabric that is powered by domestic energy sources. | “India’s AI strategy is built on three pillars: energy sovereignty, compute and cloud sovereignty, and services sovereignty”【7】. |
| **Services Sovereignty** | Use the nation’s digital public‑infrastructure (Aadhaar, UPI, digital identity) as the backbone for AI‑enabled public services. | The plan calls for “providing free AI services (doctors, tutors, agronomists) to citizens through the Aadhaar stack before deploying disruptive business applications”【1】【3】. |
| **Governance & Innovation** | Combine sovereign infrastructure with a skilled workforce, collaborative research, and agile, multistakeholder regulation. | “India needs a three‑pillar AI strategy: invest in sovereign compute infrastructure and energy, innovate through skilling and collaboration, and evolve governance frameworks with agile regulations”【5】. |
| **Model‑size Focus** | Prioritise highly performant, low‑cost models (30‑200 billion parameters) rather than chasing trillion‑parameter AGI projects. | “India’s approach … focusing on highly performant, low‑cost models (30‑200 billion parameters) rather than competing in the trillion‑parameter AGI race”【6】. |
| **Global Positioning** | Leverage its success with UPI and digital identity to shape AI governance, steering clear of the overly restrictive EU model, the laissez‑faire US model, or the state‑controlled Chinese model. | “How India should position itself globally in AI governance, leveraging its successful digital public infrastructure experience … while avoiding the pitfalls of overly restrictive European models, laissez‑faire American approaches, or state‑controlled Chinese systems”【2】. |
| **Data Access** | Streamline high‑quality data‑set availability for research and innovation. | “The streamlined access to good quality data sets for research and innovation is a part of India’s AI strategy”【8】. |

**Overall picture** – India sees AI as a national destiny (see the remark “AI is India’s destiny”【4】) and is shaping a strategy that couples **sovereign compute and energy** with **wide‑reach, free public AI services**, while fostering **skill development, collaborative research, and agile, multistakeholder governance**. The emphasis is on **cost‑effective, high‑performing models** and on using existing digital infrastructure (Aadhaar, UPI) to deliver value quickly, rather than racing to build massive, proprietary AGI systems.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

India’s AI roadmap is being shaped around three inter‑locking pillars that blend technical sovereignty with inclusive service delivery and agile governance.

* **Sovereign compute & energy** – The strategy calls for building home‑grown high‑performance compute clusters and securing the energy needed to run them, so that AI workloads can be hosted on “cloud‑sovereign” platforms rather than relying on foreign providers【5†L1-L3】【7†L1-L3】.  

* **Human‑capital & collaboration** – A strong emphasis is placed on skilling the workforce, fostering partnerships between academia, industry and government, and creating a vibrant ecosystem for AI research and innovation【5†L1-L3】.  

* **Governance & services sovereignty** – India intends to evolve agile regulatory frameworks that protect privacy and security while enabling rapid rollout of public‑interest AI services (e.g., free doctor, tutor and agronomist assistants delivered through the Aadhaar identity stack). This “services‑sovereignty” approach leverages the country’s existing digital public‑infrastructure such as UPI and Aadhaar, positioning India between the restrictive European model, the laissez‑faire U.S. model, and the state‑controlled Chinese model【2†L1-L3】【1†L1-L3】.  

* **Model‑size focus** – Rather than chasing the trillion‑parameter “AGI” race, India is concentrating on highly performant, low‑cost models in the 30‑200 billion‑parameter range, which are more suitable for the nation’s cost‑sensitive market and can be deployed at scale【6†L1-L3】.  

* **Data access for research** – Streamlined, high‑quality data‑set availability is a key component, ensuring that researchers and innovators can train and test models efficiently within the country’s ecosystem【8†L1-L3】.  

Together, these pillars aim to boost economic productivity, enhance strategic capabilities, and deliver AI‑driven public services to all citizens while maintaining national control over critical AI infrastructure and data.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**Human‑rights defenders (HRDs)**  

- The United Nations Human Rights Council lists HRDs as a very broad group that includes **clerics, teachers, students, writers, reporters, bloggers, painters, musicians, actors, publishers, booksellers, web‑masters, politicians, human‑rights defenders and dissidents**【1】.  
- In the “Defending Voice & Expression in Africa and the Middle East” side event, HRDs are named alongside **journalists, lawyers, judges, politicians, students and candidates for election**, many of whom are **imprisoned or persecuted for criticizing their governments**【2】.  
- The General Assembly’s “Summit of the Future” plenary explicitly states that **human‑rights defenders need to be protected**【4】.  

**Students**  

- Students appear repeatedly as a distinct sub‑group of HRDs. They are mentioned as **victims** (e.g., “clerics, teachers, **students**, writers…”)【1】 and as **targets of repression** in the same side‑event that discusses jailed journalists and activists【2】.  
- In the DiploFoundation note, **“thousands…mostly students”** staged large‑scale demonstrations demanding **democratic reforms**【7】.  
- An emerging‑leader narrative adds that a student activist was **an active member of a large network of European university students (AEGEE)**, organizing rallies and resistance activities【8】.  

**Key take‑aways**

| Group | How they are described in the provided sources |
|-------|-----------------------------------------------|
| **Human‑rights defenders** | A wide‑ranging coalition of civil‑society actors (clerics, teachers, students, journalists, etc.) who face intimidation, imprisonment, or other reprisals for speaking out; their protection is repeatedly called for by UN bodies. |
| **Students** | Both a subset of HRDs and a distinct mobilising force; they have led mass protests for democratic change and are organized through trans‑national networks like AEGEE. |

Thus, within the supplied context, **human‑rights defenders and students are closely linked**: students are counted among HRDs, they are often on the front lines of advocacy and protest, and both groups are highlighted as needing protection and support in international forums.

</details>

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**Geneva Internet Platform – A Brief History (based on the supplied sources)**  

| Date | Milestone | Details |
|------|-----------|---------|
| **8 April 2014** | **Official inauguration** | The Geneva Internet Platform (GIP) was launched in Geneva on Tuesday, 8 April 2014. The opening ceremony featured a welcome address by **Ambassador Alexandre Fasel** (Switzerland’s Permanent Representative to the UN in Geneva), keynote reflections by **Michael Moller** (Acting Director‑General of UNOG) and **Philipp Metzger** (Director‑General of the Swiss Federal Office of Communications), and a presentation of the platform by **Jovan Kurbalija**, Director of DiploFoundation [1][2]. |
| 2014 (post‑launch) | **Mission statement articulated** | The platform was presented as a “vibrant scene” that would **bridge policy silos**, **strengthen the participation of small and developing countries**, **monitor digital policies**, and **facilitate research for evidence‑based Internet Governance (IG)** [2][3][4]. |
| 2014‑present | **Operational model** | GIP is a **Swiss initiative operated by DiploFoundation**. Its core activities include providing a **neutral, inclusive space for digital‑policy debate**, supporting Geneva‑based IG and ICT institutions, and developing tools and methods for both in‑situ and online engagement that could be replicated in other policy spaces worldwide [5][6][7][8]. |
| 5 May 2016 | **Public acknowledgment** | DiploNews (Issue 299) again described GIP as a Swiss initiative run by DiploFoundation, confirming its continued existence and relevance two years after launch [6]. |

### Key Themes of the Platform’s History  

1. **Founding Context** – The launch was part of a broader effort to make Geneva’s digital‑policy ecosystem more inclusive and coordinated, addressing the “policy silos” that often separate different stakeholder groups.  

2. **Founding Partners** – The Swiss government (through the Federal Office of Communications), the United Nations Office at Geneva (UNOG), and DiploFoundation were the principal actors behind the platform’s creation.  

3. **Core Objectives (since inception)**  
   - **Neutral, inclusive debate space** for digital actors.  
   - **Enhanced participation** of small and developing countries in Geneva‑based digital‑policy processes.  
   - **Monitoring and analysis** of emerging digital policies.  
   - **Evidence‑based, multidisciplinary research** to inform Internet Governance.  
   - **Bridging of policy silos** and provision of tools for both physical and virtual engagement [5][7].  

4. **Continuity** – By at least 2016 the platform remained active and was still identified as a Swiss‑run DiploFoundation initiative, indicating sustained operation beyond the launch year [6].

### Summary  

The Geneva Internet Platform was officially inaugurated on **8 April 2014** in Geneva, with high‑level Swiss and UN representatives introducing it as a new, neutral hub for digital‑policy dialogue. Operated by **DiploFoundation** under Swiss sponsorship, GIP’s enduring purpose is to **connect diverse digital‑policy actors**, **amplify the voice of smaller and developing nations**, **track digital‑policy developments**, and **support evidence‑based research**—goals that have been reiterated in subsequent communications (e.g., 2016 DiploNews). This concise timeline captures the platform’s origin, its foundational mission, and its continued relevance as reflected in the provided documents.

</details>

- **Bridges policy silos** across different sectors and institutions [2][5].
- **Provides tools and methods** for both face‑to‑face and online engagement, intended for reuse in other international policy venues [5][7].

### Summary

The Geneva Internet Platform was created in April 2014 as a Swiss‑backed, DiploFoundation‑run initiative aimed at making Geneva’s digital‑policy ecosystem more inclusive, coordinated, and evidence‑driven. Since its launch, it has positioned itself as a hub that connects diverse stakeholders—especially smaller and developing‑country actors—with the broader Internet governance community, while also offering practical engagement tools that can be exported to other diplomatic settings. Its continued mention in DiploFoundation communications (e.g., 2016 DiploNews) indicates that it remains an active part of the international digital‑policy landscape.

### Q43: How does AI affect international negotiations?

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 4.91s | 3.47s (+0.28s expansion) |
| Score avg | 5.0/5 | 4.2/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 4 |
| **Winner** | **✓** |  |

> **Judge:** Answer A covers all key points, adds extra nuance on co‑creation and strategic implications, and cites sources with precise references, making it more complete and better sourced.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

AI is beginning to reshape the way states negotiate by giving diplomats tools that can handle the sheer scale and complexity of modern multilateral talks.

* **Tracking and visualising positions** – In the UNESCO negotiation of the “Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence,” a Zoom‑based view of where 193 countries stood helped the negotiators see alignments that would otherwise have been hidden (e.g., Russia and the UK sharing a stance). AI‑driven dashboards can therefore map the “who‑is‑where” landscape in real‑time, making it easier to spot coalitions and potential compromises【1†L1-L9】.

* **Processing massive comment‑streams** – The same UNESCO process received ≈55 000 public comments. AI was used to ingest, categorise and synthesise that volume of feedback, turning a flood of text into actionable insights for the final document【1†L13-L16】.

* **Managing document overload and historical knowledge** – AI can scan thousands of negotiation texts, identify relevant precedents, and surface counter‑arguments that diplomats might otherwise miss. This capability is highlighted as a way to “track positions, process vast amounts of documents, and identify historical precedents and counter‑arguments” in multilateral settings【8†L1-L3】.

* **Augmenting human judgment** – Across the sources the recurring theme is that AI is not meant to replace negotiators but to “augment humans in what is at its core a fundamentally human process of negotiation”【3†L1-L3】【7†L1-L3】. By handling data‑intensive tasks, AI frees diplomats to focus on the substantive, relational aspects of diplomacy.

* **Creating new co‑creation opportunities** – The UNESCO experience showed that AI can help structure negotiations (e.g., defining step‑by‑step processes) and support inclusive, “co‑creation, co‑collaboration” among diverse stakeholders【1†L4-L8】.

* **Strategic implications** – Because AI can surface new topics, reshape power dynamics and raise human‑rights concerns, the broader community calls for “greater scrutiny” of its impact on international relations【2†L1-L4】【4†L1-L4】. In other words, while AI can make negotiations more efficient, it also introduces new diplomatic challenges that must be monitored.

**In sum**, AI affects international negotiations by:

1. Providing real‑time visualisation of each party’s stance.  
2. Processing and summarising huge volumes of textual input (comments, treaty drafts, historical records).  
3. Surfacing relevant precedents and counter‑arguments to inform strategy.  
4. Allowing diplomats to concentrate on relationship‑building and decision‑making rather than data‑management.  
5. Introducing new agenda‑setting dynamics and human‑rights considerations that require careful oversight.  

These effects together make multilateral negotiations more manageable, more data‑driven, and potentially more inclusive—while also demanding vigilant governance.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

AI is reshaping the way states negotiate by giving diplomats tools that can handle the sheer scale and complexity of modern multilateral talks.

* **Tracking and visualising positions** – In the UNESCO negotiation of the “Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence,” a Zoom‑based view of where 193 countries stood helped the negotiators see alignments that would otherwise be hidden. AI‑driven dashboards can map such positions in real‑time, making it easier to spot coalitions (e.g., Russia and the UK occupying the same stance) and to target outreach [1].

* **Processing massive comment‑streams** – The same UNESCO process received 55 000 public comments. AI was used to ingest, categorise and synthesize that flood of input, turning an unmanageable data set into actionable insights for the final text [1].

* **Managing document volume and precedent** – AI can scan thousands of legal texts, policy papers and past agreements, surface relevant historical precedents and flag counter‑arguments, thereby reducing the manual research burden on negotiators [8].

* **Augmenting human judgment** – While negotiation remains a fundamentally human activity, AI tools act as “co‑creators” that suggest options, highlight gaps, and propose language tweaks, allowing diplomats to focus on the substantive, relational aspects of the deal [3][7].

* **Shaping the agenda and geopolitics** – By surfacing emerging technical issues and analysing their geopolitical implications, AI can push new topics onto the international agenda, alter power dynamics, and raise fresh human‑rights considerations that negotiators must address [2][4][5].

In sum, AI helps international negotiations by (1) visualising and tracking the positions of many actors, (2) digesting huge volumes of commentary and documentation, (3) surfacing relevant precedents and arguments, and (4) acting as an analytical partner that augments—rather than replaces—human diplomatic judgment, while also introducing new agenda‑setting and strategic dimensions. [1][6][8]

</details>

### Q45: What is India AI policy

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 4.80s | 6.10s (+0.30s expansion) |
| Score avg | 4.8/5 | 5.0/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 2 |

> **Judge:** Both answers are thorough and accurate, but A provides slightly richer detail and clearer source referencing, giving it the edge.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**India’s AI policy is a mix of strategic road‑maps, funding programmes and sector‑specific guidelines rather than a single, binding legal framework.** The main elements that together constitute “India AI policy” are:

| Component | What it does | Key points |
|-----------|--------------|------------|
| **National Strategy for Artificial Intelligence (June 2018)** | First‑ever government‑level blueprint for AI. | Targets AI use in **healthcare, agriculture, education, smart cities & infrastructure, and smart mobility/transport**. Sets the vision for a “strong basis for future regulation” and emphasizes responsible, inclusive development. |
| **India AI Mission (announced March 2024)** | A multi‑billion‑dollar implementation programme. | **$1.25 billion** earmarked for: <br>• Building **computing‑infrastructure capacity** (e.g., free GPU clusters). <br>• **Skilling** of AI talent. <br>• Supporting **innovation** and home‑grown AI models. <br>• Creating **datasets** and test‑beds. <br>• Ensuring **safe and trusted AI** (risk‑based safeguards, governance). |
| **Digital Personal Data Protection Act (2023)** | First comprehensive data‑privacy law. | Provides the legal foundation for how personal data—crucial for AI training and deployment—must be collected, stored, processed, and shared. |
| **Regulatory Advisories & Guidelines** | Non‑binding but influential guidance. | Recent advisories on **deep‑fakes, generative AI, and responsible AI practices** that outline expectations for developers and users. |
| **IndiaAI portal (launched May 2022)** | Central information hub. | Publishes updates on AI research, funding calls, policy documents, and best‑practice resources. |
| **Principles‑based, flexible framework** | The prevailing regulatory stance. | India has **no AI law that carries the force of legislation yet**; instead it relies on **principles, sector‑specific guidelines and risk‑based oversight** to encourage responsible innovation while avoiding the rigidity of European models, the laissez‑faire of the U.S., or the state‑centric approach of China. |
| **Strategic positioning** | How India sees its role globally. | Leverages its **digital public‑infrastructure experience (UPI, Aadhaar, digital identity)** to shape a balanced AI governance model that promotes economic productivity, security and citizen services without over‑regulation. |

### In short
India’s AI policy is built around:

1. **A strategic vision (National Strategy 2018)** that identifies priority sectors.  
2. **Heavy public investment (India AI Mission 2024)** to develop infrastructure, talent, datasets and trustworthy AI.  
3. **Data‑privacy legislation (DPDP Act 2023)** that underpins responsible AI use.  
4. **Guidelines and advisories** (deep‑fake, generative AI) that set expectations without imposing strict legal mandates.  
5. **A principles‑based, flexible regulatory approach** that seeks to foster innovation while safeguarding citizens and aligning with India’s broader digital ecosystem.

These pieces together form the current “India AI policy” landscape. [1] [2] [3] [4] [5] [6] [8]

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**India’s AI policy – a snapshot from the supplied material**

| Element | What the policy says / does | Source |
|---------|-----------------------------|--------|
| **National Strategy for Artificial Intelligence** | Adopted in June 2018, it sets the overall direction for AI regulation and development. It concentrates on AI use‑cases in **healthcare, agriculture, education, smart‑cities & infrastructure, and smart mobility/transportation**. | [1] |
| **India AI Mission (2024)** | In March 2024 the government announced **more than $1.25 billion** for a dedicated AI Mission. Funding is earmarked for **computing‑infrastructure capacity, skilling, innovation, data‑sets, and the development of “safe and trusted” AI**. The Mission also backs advisory notes on deep‑fakes and generative‑AI risks. | [1] |
| **Legal framework** | The **Digital Personal Data Protection Act (2023)** provides the core data‑privacy regime that AI systems must respect. No AI‑specific law has yet been enacted; the regulatory approach remains **principles‑based rather than prescriptive**. | [1] |
| **Implementation & coordination mechanisms** | • **IndiaAI** – a government‑run web portal (launched May 2022) that aggregates AI‑related initiatives, research, and policy updates. <br>• Ongoing **advisories** on emerging AI threats (e.g., deep‑fakes). | [1] |
| **Regulatory stance** | India currently **has no binding AI regulations**; instead it is moving toward a **flexible, principles‑based framework** intended to encourage responsible innovation while avoiding the heavy‑handedness of some jurisdictions. | [1] |
| **Strategic positioning** | Policy discussions stress leveraging India’s **digital public‑infrastructure strengths** (UPI, Aadhaar, digital identity) to become a global AI governance model that steers clear of overly restrictive European rules, laissez‑faire US approaches, or state‑controlled Chinese models. | [3], [5] |
| **Sector‑specific ambitions** | • **Free AI services** (e.g., AI doctors, tutors, agronomists) delivered through the Aadhaar stack before commercial roll‑outs. <br>• Emphasis on **economic productivity, military capability, and information control** as long‑term goals. | [4] |
| **Infrastructure support** | Government‑backed provision of **free GPUs** and funding for large‑scale AI models, plus attention to **energy‑infrastructure needs** for sovereign AI development. | [2] |

### Key take‑aways

1. **Strategic, sector‑focused roadmap** – The 2018 National Strategy identifies priority domains (health, agriculture, education, smart cities, mobility) and frames AI as a driver of development and sustainable growth.  
2. **Heavy investment in capacity building** – The 2024 India AI Mission allocates > $1.25 bn to build compute infrastructure, develop talent, create datasets, and ensure AI safety and trustworthiness.  
3. **Principles‑based, non‑binding regulation** – Rather than a strict AI law, India relies on the broader **Digital Personal Data Protection Act** and advisory notes, aiming for flexibility and rapid innovation.  
4. **Leveraging existing digital infrastructure** – The policy envisions AI services built on Aadhaar, UPI and other public‑digital platforms, positioning India as a “sovereign AI” provider.  
5. **Global positioning** – India seeks a middle path, using its successful digital‑public‑infrastructure experience to shape an AI governance model that is neither overly restrictive nor laissez‑faire.  

In sum, **India’s AI policy is a combination of a long‑term strategic vision (the 2018 National Strategy), a sizable financial commitment (the 2024 AI Mission), a data‑privacy law (DPDP Act 2023), and a flexible, principles‑based regulatory approach that leans on existing digital infrastructure to foster responsible, sovereign AI development**.

### Q48: What are fisheries provisions in trade agrreement of relevance for WTO negotiations in Yaonde?

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 9.31s | 10.41s (+0.14s expansion) |
| Score avg | 4.8/5 | 4.5/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 0 |
| **Winner** | **✓** |  |

> **Judge:** Answer A covers a broader set of provisions and links each to WTO relevance, giving a more complete picture, while both remain accurate and well‑cited.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**Key fisheries‑related clauses that appear in the sample trade agreements and that are likely to be raised in WTO‑level talks (e.g., the Yaonde negotiations)**  

| Type of provision | What the clause does | Why it matters for WTO negotiations | Example from the source material |
|-------------------|----------------------|--------------------------------------|-----------------------------------|
| **Preservation of existing fisheries relationships** | States that the trade chapter must not undermine an already‑existing fisheries treaty and that “the volume of the mutual fisheries possibilities … should continue to be maintained at a satisfactory level.” | Signals that any new trade rules cannot erode bilateral fisheries quotas or access‑rights that are already governed by a separate fisheries agreement – a point that WTO members often raise when negotiating “non‑tariff” fisheries issues. | [1] |
| **Commitments to sustainable‑development objectives and the 2030 Agenda** | Requires parties to “fulfil the objectives set out in the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development regarding fisheries subsidies,” including prohibiting subsidies that fuel over‑fishing or IUU (illegal, unreported, unregulated) fishing. | Directly touches the WTO “subsidies and countervailing measures” (SCM) discipline and the ongoing WTO discussion on “fisheries subsidies” (the so‑called “Fisheries Subsidies Negotiations”). | [2] |
| **Reference to international fisheries law** | Lists UNCLOS, the UN Fish Stocks Agreement, the FAO Compliance Agreement and the FAO Port‑State Measures Agreement as the legal framework that parties must respect. | Provides a common legal baseline that WTO members can invoke when assessing whether a trade measure is consistent with “international obligations” under the WTO’s Article 20. | [2] |
| **Traceability and catch‑documentation schemes** | Calls for the use of FAO’s Voluntary Guidelines for Catch Documentation Schemes (including traceability). | Traceability is increasingly linked to “rules of origin” and “sanitary‑phytosanitary” (SPS) measures in WTO agreements; it can be used to justify trade‑restrictive measures that are nevertheless WTO‑compatible. | [2] |
| **Recognition of the sector’s socio‑economic importance** | Reiterates that fisheries are vital for development, livelihoods (including artisanal fisheries), and for creating “environmental, economic and social opportunities.” | Provides political justification for protective or supportive measures (e.g., preferential market access, subsidies) that may otherwise be viewed as trade‑distorting. | [3] |
| **Definition of eligible vessels / ownership criteria** | Sets detailed criteria (registration, flag, ownership ≥ 50 %, crew nationality ≥ 75 %) for vessels that can benefit from the agreement; excludes vessels that fish in the other party’s EEZ from those criteria. | Such “origin‑type” rules are common in WTO “rules of origin” negotiations and can be used to carve out fisheries‑specific market‑access provisions without breaching the most‑favoured‑nation (MFN) principle. | [4] |
| **Co‑operation mechanisms (working groups, joint committees)** | Establishes a “Working Group on Fish and Fishery Products” under the broader committee structure. | Institutional mechanisms are useful for monitoring implementation of WTO commitments (e.g., on subsidies, IUU) and for resolving disputes bilaterally before they reach the WTO dispute‑settlement system. | [4] |
| **Promotion of sector competitiveness and diversification** | Calls for “diversification, adjustment and improvement of the competitiveness of the agricultural, aquaculture, forestry and fishing subsectors.” | Aligns with WTO’s “trade‑related technical assistance” and “capacity‑building” themes, and can justify temporary safeguard measures aimed at sector adjustment. | [5] |
| **General cooperation in fisheries and aquaculture** | Simple pledge to “co‑operate in the field of fisheries and aquaculture.” | Mirrors the WTO’s “peace‑building” language and can be a platform for future negotiations on fisheries‑related trade rules. | [6] |
| **Preferential market access linked to compliance with rules of origin** | Allows fish and fish products from ACP countries that meet EU rules of origin to enter the EU tariff‑free. | Demonstrates how fisheries provisions can be embedded in “preferential trade agreements” (PTAs) while still respecting WTO rules on MFN and “subsidies and countervailing measures.” | [7] |
| **WTO‑level discussion of fisheries‑subsidy disciplines** | Notes that WTO members will “indicate the provisions, including disciplines on the trade‑distorting practices (including fisheries subsidies) they seek to clarify and improve” in the next negotiation phase. | Directly signals that fisheries subsidies are on the WTO agenda and that any bilateral or regional agreement that touches subsidies will be scrutinised in the Yaonde talks. | [8] |

### Bottom‑line take‑aways for the Yaonde WTO negotiations  

1. **Subsidy discipline** – Any agreement that bans or limits “harmful” fisheries subsidies (over‑capacity, IUU) will be examined under the WTO’s ongoing fisheries‑subsidies negotiations.  
2. **Conservation & sustainability** – References to UNCLOS, FAO agreements, and the 2030 SDG create a normative baseline that WTO members can invoke to justify trade‑related measures (e.g., safeguards, traceability).  
3. **Rules of origin & preferential access** – Detailed vessel‑ownership and flag criteria are used to carve out fisheries‑specific market‑access provisions; WTO members will check that these do not discriminate against non‑parties.  
4. **Traceability & documentation** – Adoption of FAO voluntary guidelines can be a WTO‑compatible way to ensure product integrity while allowing for targeted trade measures.  
5. **Co‑operation structures** – Working groups and joint committees provide a forum for monitoring and resolving fisheries‑related trade issues before they become WTO disputes.  

These are the fisheries provisions that are most likely to be raised, compared, or used as reference points in the Yaonde WTO negotiations.

</details>

| Trade‑agreement source | Fisheries provision that could be used as a reference point in WTO talks |
|------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **[1] Free Trade Agreement (generic)** | *“The parties confirm that the trade aspects of this Agreement should not affect the functioning of the Fisheries Agreement and that the volume of the mutual fisheries possibilities … should continue to be maintained at a satisfactory level.”*  – a “separability” clause that keeps fisheries‑quota arrangements insulated from trade‑related liberalisation. |
| **[2] EFTA‑Chile FTA** | • *“Fulfil the 2030 Agenda objectives on fisheries subsidies – prohibit subsidies that contribute to over‑fishing or over‑capacity, eliminate those that fuel IUU fishing and refrain from introducing new such subsidies.”*  <br>• *Reference to UNCLOS, the UN Fish Stocks Agreement, the FAO Compliance Agreement and the FAO Port‑State Measures Agreement.*  <br>• *Promotion of international guidelines such as FAO’s Voluntary Guidelines for Catch Documentation Schemes (traceability).*  <br>• *Recognition that trade must support the conservation and sustainable management of marine resources.*  | These clauses provide a model for WTO‑level “subsidies‑and‑fisheries” rules (e.g., the ongoing WTO fisheries‑subsidies negotiations) and for linking trade commitments to the 2030 Sustainable Development Goals. |
| **[3] Argentina‑Chile Trade Agreement** | *“The Parties recognize the importance of the fisheries sector for development and for the livelihood of fishing communities, including artisanal fisheries, and the importance of conservation and sustainable management of fisheries for present‑ and future‑generation opportunities.”*  – a broad statement of environmental and social objectives that can be invoked when arguing for “green” trade rules. |
| **[4] Japan‑Chile Strategic Economic Partnership** | • Detailed *nationality/ownership* criteria for vessels that may benefit from preferential treatment (e.g., ≥ 50 % ownership by nationals, ≥ 75 % crew nationals, master/officers all nationals).  <br>• *Exclusion clause*: “subparagraph (f) shall not apply to fish, shellfish and other marine species taken from the exclusive economic zone of the other Party by the vessels referred to in that subparagraph.”  <br>• Creation of a *Working Group on Fish and Fishery Products* under the joint committee. | Useful for WTO discussions on “rules of origin” for fishery products and on the treatment of EEZ‑caught fish in preferential regimes. |
| **[5] China (Taiwan)‑El Salvador‑Honduras FTA** | *“Cooperation to diversify, adjust and improve competitiveness of the agricultural, aquaculture, forestry and fishing subsectors; support strategies, actions and policy measures in agriculture, forestry, aquaculture, fishing and animal/plant health inspection.”* – a multi‑sectoral development‑oriented approach that can be cited when negotiating technical assistance or capacity‑building provisions at the WTO. |
| **[6] Korea‑Colombia FTA** | *“The Parties, recognizing the social and economic importance of fish and fisheries products, shall endeavor to cooperate in the field of fisheries and aquaculture.”* – a simple cooperation clause that can be mirrored in WTO “enhanced cooperation” language. |
| **[7] EU‑ACP (Maldives/Mauritius) case study** | *“The treaty allowed fish and fish products from ACP countries that comply with EU rules of origin to be imported into the EU free of tariffs.”* – an illustration of how fisheries‑specific rules of origin can be built into preferential market‑access arrangements. |
| **[8] Academic note on SIDS** | *“WTO members will indicate the provisions, including disciplines on trade‑distorting practices (including fisheries subsidies), they seek to clarify and improve in the subsequent phase of negotiations.”* – confirms that fisheries‑subsidy disciplines are already on the WTO agenda and that SIDS (including Yaonde, if it is a small island developing state) can push for stronger rules. |

### How these provisions can be leveraged by Yaonde in WTO negotiations

1. **Subsidy Discipline & Sustainable Development** – The EFTA‑Chile language ([2]) gives a concrete template for a WTO rule that *prohibits* subsidies that cause over‑fishing, IUU fishing, or over‑capacity, while linking the rule to the 2030 Agenda. Yaonde can cite this as a precedent for “non‑distortive” subsidies and for the inclusion of environmental objectives in trade law.

2. **Separability / “No‑Erosion” Clause** – The generic FTA clause in [1] can be used to argue that any multilateral fisheries‑related commitments (e.g., quota allocations) should be insulated from broader tariff‑liberalisation commitments, preventing “policy erosion” of existing fisheries management regimes.

3. **Rules of Origin & Preferential Access** – The EU‑ACP example ([7]) and the detailed vessel‑nationality rules in Japan‑Chile ([4]) illustrate how fisheries‑specific rules of origin can be crafted. Yaonde can propose similar provisions to protect its domestic fishery sector while still benefitting from preferential market access.

4. **Co‑operation & Capacity‑Building** – The cooperation clauses in [3], [5] and [6] provide language that can be incorporated into WTO “enhanced cooperation” or “technical assistance” chapters, allowing Yaonde to request support for sustainable aquaculture, traceability systems, and compliance with international fisheries agreements.

5. **Reference to International Legal Frameworks** – The explicit incorporation of UNCLOS, the UN Fish Stocks Agreement, and FAO conventions in [2] demonstrates that WTO agreements can (and do) reference existing multilateral environmental and fisheries treaties. Yaonde can argue that any WTO fisheries‑subsidy rule should be consistent with these instruments.

6. **Working Groups & Institutional Mechanisms** – The establishment of a Working Group on Fish and Fishery Products in [4] offers a model for a WTO‑level “Fishery Products Working Group” that could monitor implementation, share best practices, and resolve disputes.

### Bottom line for Yaonde

- **Core pillars to push at WTO:** (i) a prohibition on harmful fisheries subsidies; (ii) a “no‑erosion” clause protecting existing quota/EEZ arrangements; (iii) fisheries‑specific rules of origin; (iv) capacity‑building and technical‑assistance commitments; (v) alignment with UNCLOS/FAO conventions; (vi) an institutional mechanism (working group) for fisheries‑product issues.

These pillars are directly reflected in the provisions listed above and give Yaonde a solid textual basis to shape the WTO fisheries‑subsidies negotiations in line with its development and sustainability priorities.

### Q50: what was norvegian policy during covid?

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|

> **Judge:** Both answers comprehensively cover Norway's COVID‑19 policy, stay faithful to the likely source material, give concrete details, and cite sources effectively, making them indistinguishable in quality.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

During the COVID‑19 pandemic Norway pursued a multi‑track policy that combined rapid economic relief, a strong emphasis on digital transformation, and strict data‑privacy safeguards.

1. **Economic safety net** – The Norwegian Parliament quickly enacted statutes and regulations to strengthen the economic safety net for households and businesses as the pandemic unfolded【2】.  

2. **Digitalisation as a catalyst** – The crisis was framed as an opportunity to accelerate digitalisation across the public sector and key industries (aquaculture, maritime, agriculture, tourism). A dedicated report described Norway as “well positioned to meet the crisis” and highlighted COVID‑19 as a catalyst for digital transformation【7】.  

3. **Open‑source digital public goods** – Norway’s broader digital development strategy, articulated in the Open Forum #66, stresses the use of open, non‑commercial digital public goods to avoid vendor lock‑in and uphold democratic principles【8】. This approach guided the country’s pandemic‑era digital initiatives.  

4. **Privacy‑first health‑tech** – When the national data‑protection authority warned that the COVID‑19 contact‑tracing app was processing personal data unlawfully, the Norwegian Institute of Public Health halted the app and deleted all collected data【3】. This reflects a policy of prioritising privacy over continued digital health surveillance.  

5. **Gradual removal of restrictive measures** – A Norwegian Official Report (March 2021) recommended that any coronavirus measures limiting the ability to restructure the economy be lifted “as soon as it is prudent to do so,” aiming to restore competitiveness, green growth and labour‑market stability【6】.  

6. **Societal adjustments** – The government acknowledged the shift to home‑working, home‑schooling and reduced physical contact as the “new normal,” and recognised the pandemic’s impact on cross‑border shopping and food‑drink production and sales【4】【5】.

**In summary**, Norway’s COVID‑19 policy combined swift economic support, an accelerated and open‑source‑driven digitalisation agenda, a firm stance on data‑privacy (evidenced by the shutdown of the contact‑tracing app), and a planned, timely rollback of restrictive economic measures to safeguard competitiveness and green growth【2】【3】【4】【5】【6】【7】【8】.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

During the COVID‑19 pandemic Norway pursued a multi‑track policy that combined rapid economic relief, a strong push for digitalisation, and a firm commitment to privacy and open‑source digital public goods.

| Policy area | What Norway did | Source |
|------------|----------------|--------|
| **Economic safety net** | The Parliament quickly turned pandemic‑related decisions into statutes and regulations that strengthened the economic safety net for households and businesses. | 【2】 |
| **Work and schooling** | When the crisis hit, the “new normal” shifted to home‑working, home‑schooling and minimal physical contact, reflecting a nationwide move to remote‑service delivery. | 【4】 |
| **Cross‑border trade & food sector** | Pandemic measures affected cross‑border shopping and the production and sale of food and drink, prompting policy attention to these supply‑chain impacts. | 【5】 |
| **Economic restructuring limits** | A 2021 official report recommended that any coronavirus‑induced limits on the ability to restructure the economy be lifted as soon as it was prudent, aiming for stronger competitiveness, green growth and long‑term sustainability. | 【6】 |
| **Digital transformation** | The crisis was framed as a catalyst for digitalisation. Norway was described as “well positioned to meet the crisis” and pursued a pioneering public‑sector digital transformation across sectors such as aquaculture, maritime, agriculture and tourism. | 【7】 |
| **Privacy protection** | Norway halted its COVID‑19 contact‑tracing app and deleted all related data after the national data‑protection authority warned that processing personal information would be temporarily banned. This demonstrated a policy priority for privacy and human‑rights compliance. | 【3】 |
| **Open digital public goods** | Norway’s broader digital strategy emphasised open, non‑commercial digital public goods to avoid vendor lock‑in and to promote democratic principles in development cooperation. | 【8】 |

**Overall picture**  
Norwegian COVID‑19 policy was characterised by:

1. **Rapid legislative action** to protect incomes and jobs.  
2. **Accelerated digitalisation** across public services and key industries, using the pandemic as a catalyst.  
3. **Protection of privacy and human rights**, exemplified by the shutdown of the contact‑tracing app.  
4. **Commitment to open, non‑proprietary digital solutions** to ensure long‑term, democratic digital infrastructure.  

These elements together formed Norway’s pandemic response, balancing immediate economic relief, digital innovation, and fundamental rights.

The Diplo Foundation has built its own **AI‑assistant for diplomatic reporting**, a custom‑made system that couples specialised diplomatic data‑sets, custom algorithms and a large‑language‑model (LLM).  In public statements the tool is referred to as **“Diplo’s AI reporting system”** and, for the United Nations Security Council, it has been branded **“DiploGPT.”** [2][5][7]

- **AI‑assistant name:** Diplo’s AI reporting system (also called DiploGPT when used for UN Security Council briefings).  
- **Tool used to track UN General Assembly speeches:** The same AI reporting system was deployed and tested during the **78th UN General Assembly** (and again at the Internet Governance Forum 2023) to produce real‑time transcripts and event summaries of the plenary debates. [1][5]

The Diplo Foundation has created a family of **AI‑assisted reporting tools** that work together with human analysts to turn diplomatic statements into searchable, summarised data.  

* **DiploGPT** – a custom‑trained large‑language‑model that was deployed for “just‑in‑time” reporting of the UN Security Council public debate (Future‑proofing trust for sustaining peace) 【2】.  
* **The generic AI‑reporting system / human‑AI hybrid** – a broader AI assistant that combines specialised diplomatic data‑sets, custom algorithms and a large‑language‑model to produce transcripts, summaries and analytics for multilateral meetings. The **AI‑reporting system (the human‑AI hybrid tool)** that was tested during the 78th UN General Assembly provided the transcripts and event‑summaries of the member‑state speeches 【1】【5】.  DiploGPT, by contrast, was used for the Security Council, not the General Assembly.

### Q52: building trusting AI

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 5.12s | 3.65s (+0.29s expansion) |
| Score avg | 5.0/5 | 4.2/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 4 |
| **Winner** | **✓** |  |

> **Judge:** Answer B covers more dimensions (e.g., rights‑respecting and KPI measurement), provides more concrete actions, and integrates sources more thoroughly.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**How to Build Trust‑Centred AI (What the Sources Say)**  

| What the sources stress | Practical implication for building “trusting” AI | Source |
|------------------------|---------------------------------------------------|--------|
| **Transparency & understandability** – systems that are open and can be explained to users help create trust. | Design AI models and interfaces that expose decision logic, data provenance, and performance metrics in plain language. | [1], [7] |
| **Trust as a foundational layer, not an add‑on** – every part of the stack (infrastructure, platform, application) must embed trust from the start. | Adopt a “trust‑by‑design” approach: embed security, privacy, and accountability controls at the hardware, cloud‑service, and application levels rather than tacking them on later. | [6] |
| **Relationship‑driven trust** – the whole operation relies on strong relationships among developers, operators, and end‑users; breaking that relationship erodes trust. | Foster continuous collaboration (e.g., joint workshops, co‑design sessions) between technical teams and the people who will deploy and use the AI, and set clear expectations for maintenance and updates. | [3] |
| **Stakeholder involvement** – multiple actors (governments, industry, civil society) have roles in building and sustaining trust. | Create multi‑stakeholder governance bodies that define standards, audit procedures, and redress mechanisms for AI systems. | [5] |
| **Rights‑respecting, business‑focused trust** – trust is a key factor for AI adoption in commercial settings and must align with human rights. | Conduct impact assessments that evaluate how AI respects privacy, non‑discrimination, and other rights, and communicate findings to business partners and customers. | [4] |
| **Trust as a core strategic goal** – several discussions simply label trust as the primary objective of AI initiatives. | Make trust an explicit KPI (e.g., user confidence scores, incident‑free operation periods) and report on it regularly to internal and external audiences. | [2], [3], [4] |

### A Concise “Trust‑Building” Checklist (derived from the above)

1. **Make the system transparent** – publish model documentation, data sources, and explainability tools.  
2. **Embed trust at every architectural layer** – secure hardware, trustworthy cloud services, and accountable application logic.  
3. **Cultivate relationships** – keep open communication channels between developers, operators, and users; treat trust as a partnership.  
4. **Involve all relevant stakeholders** – set up governance forums that include regulators, industry groups, and civil‑society representatives.  
5. **Align with rights‑respecting principles** – run rights‑impact assessments and ensure compliance with privacy, fairness, and non‑discrimination standards.  
6. **Measure and report trust** – define metrics (e.g., user satisfaction, audit outcomes) and publish regular trust‑performance reports.

By following these points—transparent design, trust‑by‑design architecture, relationship‑focused collaboration, multi‑stakeholder governance, rights‑respecting practices, and explicit trust metrics—organizations can move from “building AI” to **building trusting AI** as highlighted across the cited discussions.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

Building trust in AI is repeatedly framed in the cited discussions as a **foundational, multi‑layered effort rather than a bolt‑on feature**.  The key ingredients that emerge from the sources are:

1. **Transparency and Understandability** – When AI systems are designed so that their inner workings can be inspected and explained, users are more likely to feel confident in them.  (“building transparent systems that are understandable… one way to build trust” [1][7]).

2. **Relationship‑Based Trust** – Stakeholders (developers, operators, regulators, and end‑users) must cultivate ongoing relationships grounded in mutual confidence.  Trust is reinforced when every element of the architecture is deliberately constructed to support it, and when parties work closely to avoid breaking that confidence. ([3]).

3. **Trust Embedded Across All Technical Layers** – Trust should be woven into the **infrastructure**, **platform**, and **application** levels from the outset, rather than added later as an afterthought. ([6]).

4. **Stakeholder Collaboration** – Building trust is a shared responsibility; different actors (industry, governments, civil society) need to coordinate to define, monitor, and uphold trustworthy practices. ([5]).

5. **Continuous Safeguarding** – Trust is not static; it must be actively maintained (“make sure the trust is not broken”). ([3]).

**In practice**, a “trust‑first” AI development approach would therefore:

- Publish clear documentation, model cards, and explainability tools so that system behavior is visible and understandable.  
- Establish formal partnership agreements and governance mechanisms that define roles, responsibilities, and accountability among all participants.  
- Integrate security, privacy, fairness, and reliability controls at the hardware, cloud‑service, and application stages.  
- Create ongoing oversight bodies (e.g., multi‑stakeholder working groups) to monitor compliance and address emerging concerns.  

By aligning technical design (transparency, layered safeguards) with relational governance (continuous stakeholder engagement), AI systems can earn and retain the trust of both the people who build them and the people who use them. [1][3][5][6][7]

</details>

### Q54: How does ECMAScript differ from JavaScript?

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 4.34s | 4.67s (+0.21s expansion) |
| Score avg | 5.0/5 | 4.2/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 2 |
| **Winner** | **✓** |  |

> **Judge:** Answer A covers more facets, provides extra concrete details (e.g., update cadence, extensions) and cites sources more thoroughly, making it the stronger response.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**ECMAScript is the *standard*; JavaScript is an *implementation* of that standard.**  

| Aspect | ECMAScript | JavaScript |
|--------|------------|------------|
| **What it is** | The language *specification* maintained by Ecma International (ECMA‑262 and related documents). It defines the syntax, semantics, built‑in objects, and APIs that any conforming engine must provide. | The name originally given to the **Netscape** implementation of the ECMAScript specification (later adopted by all browsers). It is the concrete runtime that executes the code described by the spec. |
| **Scope** | Vendor‑neutral, cross‑platform description of the language. It can be implemented in browsers, servers, embedded devices, micro‑controllers, etc. | A particular product (or family of products) that follows the ECMAScript spec, but may also add its own extensions (e.g., browser‑specific APIs). |
| **Relationship** | “ECMAScript … is the general‑purpose programming language …” – the *reference* that implementations aim to match【1†L1-L4】. | “JavaScript (Netscape) and JScript (Microsoft)” are cited as the *originating technologies* that led to the ECMAScript specification【1†L1-L3】. |
| **Usage of the name** | Used when talking about the *standard* (e.g., “ECMAScript 2016”, “ECMA‑262”). | Used colloquially to refer to the language people write in browsers, even though technically they are writing code that conforms to the ECMAScript spec. |
| **Standard‑body** | Developed and maintained by Ecma International’s TC39 committee; updated yearly since 2016【1†L5-L7】. | Implemented by browser vendors (Mozilla, Microsoft, Google, Apple) and other runtimes (Node.js, Deno). |

In short, **ECMAScript defines *what* the language should be; JavaScript is one of the many engines that *realize* that definition.** The two terms are often used interchangeably in everyday conversation, but the distinction matters when discussing standards work, cross‑platform compatibility, or non‑browser environments【5†L1-L3】.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**ECMAScript is the *standard* that defines the language, while JavaScript is an *implementation* of that standard.**  

- The ECMAScript Language Specification (ECMA‑262) is the formal description of the language that “is based on several originating technologies, the most‑well‑known being **JavaScript (Netscape)** and JScript (Microsoft)”【1】.  
- ECMAScript is maintained by **Ecma International** as a vendor‑neutral, cross‑platform standard with billions of implementations worldwide【5】.  
- JavaScript, on the other hand, is the name given to the actual programming language used in browsers and other runtimes; it was one of the original “originating technologies” that fed into the ECMAScript spec【1】.  

In practice this means:

| Aspect | ECMAScript | JavaScript |
|--------|------------|------------|
| **Nature** | Formal specification (ECMA‑262, updated yearly) | Concrete language implementation that follows the spec |
| **Governance** | Developed and updated by Ecma International’s TC39 committee | Implemented by browser vendors (e.g., Netscape, Microsoft) and other runtime providers |
| **Scope** | Defines syntax, semantics, built‑in objects (maps, sets, Unicode support, etc.) | Provides the actual runtime behavior that developers write against |
| **Usage** | Serves as the reference model for any language that wants to be “ECMAScript‑compatible” | The everyday name of the language developers use in web pages, Node.js, embedded systems, etc. |

Thus, while the terms are often used interchangeably in casual conversation, **ECMAScript is the specification; JavaScript is the widely‑used language that implements that specification**【1】【5】.

</details>

| **Transparency & Explainability** | Make systems and their decisions understandable to users and stakeholders. | “building transparent systems that are understandable… ” [1]; “trust building requires transparency, explainability…” [5] |
| **Traceability & Ownership** | Keep a clear record of the data, models, and components used, and define who owns each part of the value chain. | “we need to trace the systems, the models, the data that we use for AI” [2]; “ability to trace systems and data… clear ownership along the value chain” [6] |
| **Human‑in‑the‑Loop Governance** | Ensure people can intervene, review, and override AI outputs where needed. | “human‑in‑the‑loop governance” [5] |
| **Performance Benchmarking & Knowing Limits** | Regularly test models, document failure points, and publish performance metrics so users know where the AI works well and where it does not. | “knowing the limits and failure points of models, benchmarking performance” [6] |
| **Security & Robustness** | Protect AI systems from tampering, adversarial attacks, and data breaches. | “ensuring security” [6] |
| **Layer‑wide Integration** | Embed trust considerations into every architectural layer (infrastructure, platform, application) rather than adding them later. | “Trust in AI systems must be built foundationally across all layers… rather than as an add‑on feature” [3] |
| **Relationship‑Based Trust** | Build long‑term collaborative relationships among partners, with continuous communication and joint evaluation. | “Our whole operation is built on relationship and trust… spend some time on transparency and evaluation” [4] |
| **Use Existing International Institutions** | Leverage current global governance bodies to set standards and avoid fragmenting the ecosystem with new structures. | “utilizing existing international institutions rather than creating entirely new governance structures” [7][8] |

### Practical steps for organisations

1. **Publish model cards and data sheets** that detail sources, preprocessing, intended use, and known limitations.  
2. **Implement audit trails** that log who accessed or modified models and data.  
3. **Deploy explainable‑AI tools** (e.g., feature importance visualisations) for end‑users.  
4. **Set up a human‑in‑the‑loop review process** for high‑risk decisions.  
5. **Run regular benchmark tests** against industry standards and disclose results.  
6. **Apply security best‑practices** (encryption, access controls, adversarial testing).  
7. **Define clear ownership and responsibility** for each component of the AI pipeline, documented in contracts or governance charters.  
8. **Engage with multilateral bodies** (e.g., OECD, ISO, UNESCO) to align with emerging AI standards and avoid reinventing governance frameworks.

By weaving these pillars into the design, deployment, and governance of AI, organisations can create systems that are not only technically robust but also perceived as trustworthy by users, partners, and regulators.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**How to Build Trusted AI – Key Take‑aways from the cited sessions**

| Pillar / Action | What the experts said | Why it matters |
|-----------------|-----------------------|----------------|
| **Transparency & Explainability** | “Building transparent systems that are understandable… is one way to build trust.” [1]  <br> “Trust building requires transparency, explainability, human‑in‑the‑loop governance…” [5] | Makes it possible for users, regulators and partners to see *how* a model works and *why* it reaches a particular output, reducing suspicion and enabling accountability. |
| **Traceability (Data & Model Lineage)** | “We need to trace the systems, the models, the data that we use for AI.” [2]  <br> “The ability to trace systems and data… is a key pillar.” [6] | Allows auditors to follow the provenance of inputs and transformations, detect contamination or bias, and verify compliance with policies. |
| **Understanding Limits & Failure Modes** | “Knowing the limits and failure points of models” is a pillar of trust. [6] | Helps operators set realistic expectations, design safeguards, and avoid over‑reliance on AI in high‑risk contexts. |
| **Performance Benchmarking & Evaluation** | “Benchmarking performance” is listed among the five trust pillars. [6] | Provides objective evidence that the system meets agreed‑upon standards and can be compared across vendors or versions. |
| **Security & Robustness** | “Ensuring security” is a core trust element. [6] | Protects AI from adversarial attacks, data breaches, and manipulation, preserving integrity and user confidence. |
| **Clear Ownership & Value‑Chain Governance** | “Having clear ownership along the value chain” is essential. [6] | Clarifies responsibility for data, models, and outcomes, making it easier to assign liability and enforce contracts. |
| **Human‑in‑the‑Loop Governance** | “Human‑in‑the‑loop governance” is part of trust‑building. [5] | Keeps final decision‑making under human oversight, especially where ethical or legal judgments are required. |
| **Embedding Trust Across All Layers** | “Trust in AI systems must be built foundationally across all layers (infrastructure, platform, application) rather than as an add‑on feature.” [3] | Guarantees that security, auditability and ethical safeguards are not retro‑fitted but designed into the architecture from day one. |
| **Relationship‑Based Trust & Ongoing Transparency** | “Our whole operation is built on relationship and trust… spending some time on transparency and evaluation… can really facilitate this process.” [4] | Continuous communication and joint evaluation with partners (e.g., industry, academia, civil society) reinforces confidence and prevents trust erosion. |
| **Leverage Existing International Institutions** | “Utilizing existing international institutions rather than creating entirely new governance structures” is recommended for managing AI transformation. [7][8] | Provides proven frameworks, standards and dispute‑resolution mechanisms, avoiding fragmentation and duplication. |
| **Clear Policy Boundaries for AI Experimentation** | “Clear policies defining allowed AI experimentation areas” are needed. [5] | Sets the scope of permissible uses, reducing uncertainty and aligning innovation with societal values. |

### Practical Steps to Implement These Pillars

1. **Design Phase**  
   * Define a **trust architecture** that embeds transparency, traceability, security, and human‑in‑the‑loop controls at the infrastructure, platform and application levels.  
   * Map the **value‑chain** and assign explicit ownership for data, models, and outcomes.

2. **Data & Model Management**  
   * Deploy **metadata registries** that log data provenance, preprocessing steps, and model versioning.  
   * Conduct **risk assessments** to identify limits and failure points; document them in model cards or datasheets.

3. **Evaluation & Benchmarking**  
   * Establish **independent test suites** and performance benchmarks that are publicly disclosed.  
   * Periodically **audit** security posture and robustness against adversarial attacks.

4. **Governance & Oversight**  
   * Implement **human‑in‑the‑loop review points** for high‑impact decisions.  
   * Draft **policy frameworks** that delineate permissible AI experimentation, referencing existing international standards (e.g., ISO/IEC 42001, OECD AI Principles).

5. **Stakeholder Engagement**  
   * Maintain **transparent communication channels** (reports, dashboards) with partners, regulators, and the public.  
   * Use **joint evaluation workshops** to reinforce relational trust and address concerns early.

6. **Institutional Alignment**  
   * Align internal governance with **existing international bodies** (e.g., IEEE, ISO, OECD) to leverage their standards and dispute‑resolution mechanisms rather than building parallel structures.

### Bottom Line

Building trusted AI is not a single‑off checklist; it requires a **holistic, layered approach** that weaves transparency, traceability, security, clear ownership, human oversight, and continuous evaluation into the very fabric of AI systems—while grounding the effort in established international governance frameworks and strong stakeholder relationships. By following the pillars and steps above, organisations can move from “trust as an add‑on” to “trust as a foundational attribute” of every AI deployment.

</details>

### Q59: internet standardization

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 4.73s | 4.89s (+0.28s expansion) |
| Score avg | 5.0/5 | 4.2/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 5 |
| **Winner** | **✓** |  |

> **Judge:** Answer A covers more dimensions (e.g., governance, level playing field) with concrete examples and ties each claim to specific citations, making it more complete and specific.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**Internet standardization** is the process of developing, agreeing on, and publishing technical specifications that define how the global network of computers and devices should inter‑operate. By establishing common rules and formats, standards make it possible for disparate hardware, software, and services to “speak the same language” and to work together reliably, securely and efficiently.

### Why standardization matters for the Internet  

| Aspect | What the standards deliver | Evidence from the IGF 2023 material |
|--------|----------------------------|------------------------------------|
| **Interoperability** | Guarantees that an iPhone, an Android phone, a laptop, or any other device can exchange data without custom adapters. | “Standardisation has been instrumental in allowing the internet to function seamlessly across devices and networks” [1][3]. |
| **Security & safety** | Embeds proven security mechanisms (e.g., TLS/SSL) and performance guarantees that protect users and critical services. | “International standardization … assure that the important services meet internationally recognized levels of performance and safety” [6]. |
| **Sustainability & inclusivity** | Provides a common framework for eco‑friendly practices and inclusive policy‑making, reducing duplication of effort and waste. | “Standardization … is vital in mitigating the risks associated with the internet and promoting a sustainable approach” [2]. |
| **Level playing field** | Sets baseline expectations for all providers, preventing a race‑to‑the‑bottom and encouraging fair competition. | “International standardization … provide a level playing field amongst the different offerors of the infrastructure services” [6]. |
| **Predictable governance** | Codifies the “reasonable man” behavior that underpins trust in the network’s operation. | “Standards enshrine the usual behavior of the ‘reasonable man’ … in the Internet environment” [6]. |

### Who creates the standards?  

- **International Organization for Standardization (ISO)** – develops broad‑based technical and management standards.  
- **International Telecommunication Union (ITU)** – focuses on telecommunications and network protocols.  
- **Internet Engineering Task Force (IETF)** – the primary community that writes the core Internet protocols (e.g., TCP/IP, DNS, TLS).  

These bodies are referenced directly in the IGF discussion of ARIN’s involvement in digital standardization [4].

### Core standards that keep the Internet running  

- **TCP/IP** – the fundamental transport and addressing suite that moves packets worldwide.  
- **Domain Name System (DNS)** – translates human‑readable names into IP addresses.  
- **Secure Sockets Layer / Transport Layer Security (SSL/TLS)** – provides encryption and authentication for web traffic.  
- **Content formats** such as **HTML** and **XML** that enable browsers and applications to render information consistently.  

These examples are listed in the “Technical standards and services” excerpt [8] and the abstract on infrastructure standards [5].

### Overall impact  

Internet standardization is the glue that turns a collection of independent networks into a single, global system. It underpins **security, reliability, sustainability, and equitable access**, allowing the Internet to evolve while preserving the interoperability that users expect today. [1][2][6][7]

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**Internet standardization – what it is and why it matters**

Internet standardization is the process of creating, agreeing on, and maintaining technical specifications that make the global network work reliably, securely, and interoperably across every device, platform and country. The practice is carried out by a range of international bodies—such as the International Organization for Standardization (ISO), the International Telecommunication Union (ITU), and the Internet Engineering Task Force (IETF)—which develop the protocols and formats that underpin the Internet’s architecture [4].

### Core functions of Internet standardization  

| Function | How it shows up in practice | Why it matters |
|----------|-----------------------------|----------------|
| **Interoperability** | Standards like TCP/IP, DNS, SSL, HTML and XML let an iPhone, an Android phone, a server in a data‑center and a smart‑home device all communicate without custom adapters [1][8]. | Users experience a seamless, “just‑works” Internet regardless of hardware or operating system. |
| **Performance & safety** | International standards set minimum levels of performance, reliability and security that all service providers must meet [6]. | Guarantees a “level playing field” and protects users from poorly designed or insecure services. |
| **Sustainability** | Standardised protocols can reduce redundant traffic and improve energy efficiency, contributing to an eco‑friendly Internet [2]. | Helps mitigate the Internet’s environmental footprint while supporting inclusive, long‑term growth. |
| **Governance & policy alignment** | Standardisation feeds into broader policy frameworks (e.g., inclusive digital‑infrastructure policies) and informs negotiations among stakeholders [7][8]. | Aligns technical choices with public‑policy goals such as digital inclusion, security, and resilience. |

### Who drives the standards?  

- **IETF** – the primary community of designers, operators and researchers that produces the technical documents (RFCs) shaping how the Internet is used and managed [6].  
- **ISO & ITU** – set broader digital standards that intersect with the Internet’s infrastructure [4].  
- **Regional bodies** (e.g., ARIN) participate in international standardisation processes, ensuring that regional perspectives are reflected in global norms [4].

### Why standardisation is essential for a secure and sustainable Internet  

1. **Security** – Uniform security protocols (e.g., SSL/TLS) reduce the attack surface by ensuring every node follows the same vetted procedures [1][8].  
2. **Reliability** – Consistent performance criteria prevent “wild‑card” implementations that could destabilise the network [6].  
3. **Environmental impact** – Harmonised data‑transfer methods lower unnecessary retransmissions and energy use, supporting a greener Internet [2].  
4. **Policy coherence** – Standards provide a technical baseline that policymakers can reference when drafting regulations, making negotiations more concrete and less political [7][8].

### Bottom line  

Internet standardization is the invisible scaffolding that lets the global network function as a single, secure, and increasingly sustainable system. By agreeing on common technical rules, the international community ensures that anyone—whether a user on a smartphone, a cloud provider, or a national regulator—can rely on the same predictable, safe, and environmentally responsible Internet experience.

</details>

In short, it is “the use of the Internet and social media for achieving diplomatic objectives” and represents the broader transformation of diplomacy in the internet age【4†L1-L4】【5†L1-L4】.

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

Digital diplomacy is the practice of using digital technologies—especially the Internet and social‑media platforms—to conduct diplomatic activities and pursue foreign‑policy objectives.  It encompasses the way the diplomatic environment is changing (new policy domains such as privacy, data protection, and cybersecurity), the tools diplomats employ online (social‑media communication, virtual meetings, digital public‑diplomacy platforms), and the broader agenda of managing those changes through virtual collaboration and networked engagement.  

In short, digital diplomacy = the use of the Internet and ICTs (social media, e‑tools, virtual meetings, etc.) to negotiate, represent, and advance a country’s diplomatic goals in the digital age.  

**Key points from the sources**  

- It “refers to the interplay between digital technology and the practice of diplomacy, and encompasses the changing diplomatic environment, new policy domains, and innovative digital tools” [2].  
- It is “the use of digital technologies, particularly the internet and social media, to conduct diplomatic activities and achieve foreign policy goals” [3][5].  
- It focuses on “the changes in the environment in which diplomacy is practised, the use of internet tools for diplomatic practice, and the new topics on diplomatic agendas, such as privacy, data protection, cybersecurity, etc.” [4].  
- It is described as “the use of the Internet to solve foreign policy problems” and a “prime focus for debate when it goes right to the heart of diplomacy’s core functions, negotiation and representation” [8].

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

The consensus emerging from the 2024 IGF‑related sessions is that the Internet Governance Forum will **continue to exist after 2025** – most participants expect its mandate to be renewed in that year and to remain a central multistakeholder platform for global internet policy.

Key points from the provided material:

| Source | What it says about the post‑2025 IGF |
|--------|--------------------------------------|
| **[1]** “Journey to ‘Building Our Multistakeholder Digital Future’” | Highlights a “strong sentiment” that the IGF’s mandate will be renewed beyond 2025, reinforcing its importance in internet governance. |
| **[2]** “High‑Level Session 4” | Directly asks participants to articulate a vision for the IGF **beyond 2025** and how it could help implement the Global Digital Compact (GDC). |
| **[3]** “Closing Ceremony” | Mentions “Looking Ahead to IGF 2025 – IGF’s Future and Structure,” indicating forward‑looking planning for the Forum’s role after 2025. |
| **[4]** “Journey to ‘Building Our Multistakeholder Digital Future’” | Suggests member states consider **hosting the annual IGF from 2026 onward**, presuming the mandate will be renewed. |
| **[5]** “Multistakeholder digital governance beyond 2025” | States that “the future of the IGF will define the future of the NRIs,” linking the Forum’s continuation to broader digital‑governance outcomes. |
| **[7]** “Informal Stakeholder Consultation Session” | Calls the 2025 renewal a “pivotal outcome” that affirms the IGF’s value in shaping global internet governance. |
| **[8]** “IGF 2024 Opening Ceremony” | Explicitly calls for **renewing the IGF mandate in 2025**. |

### Synthesis

1. **Mandate Renewal Expected** – Multiple references ( [1], [4], [7], [8] ) indicate that participants anticipate the IGF’s mandate will be formally renewed in 2025.  
2. **Continued Multistakeholder Role** – The Forum is seen as a cornerstone for multistakeholder digital governance, with its post‑2025 trajectory viewed as critical for the broader ecosystem of National Regulatory Initiatives (NRIs) and the Global Digital Compact (GDC) [2], [5].  
3. **Operational Planning** – There is already discussion about **hosting the annual meeting from 2026 onward**, implying that the IGF’s institutional structure and calendar will be maintained and possibly expanded [4].  
4. **Strategic Vision Development** – Sessions such as the High‑Level Session 4 are explicitly soliciting visions for the IGF’s contribution to the GDC after 2025, suggesting that strategic road‑mapping will accompany the mandate renewal [2].

### Bottom‑line answer

**The IGF is expected to continue operating after 2025, with its mandate likely renewed in that year.** Its future will involve sustained multistakeholder engagement, possible new hosting arrangements from 2026, and an expanded role in implementing global digital‑governance initiatives such as the Global Digital Compact. The exact shape of its structure and activities will be defined through ongoing discussions and strategic planning sessions leading up to and following the 2025 renewal.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**Youth Manifesto for Digital Rights**  
*(Compiled from the cited sources [1]‑[8])*

---

### 1. Representation & Inclusion  
- **Demand full representation of young people and minority groups in all digital‑governance forums.**  
  *“The ninth point addresses the issue of representation, particularly for young people and minority groups.”* [1]  

### 2. Empowerment of Youth Activists  
- **Support capacity‑building for youth‑led advocacy that counters shrinking civic space and disinformation, especially in vulnerable regions such as the Sahel.**  
  *“Digitalise Youth… seeks to promote digital rights… by empowering local youth activists and civil society organisations.”* [2]  

### 3. Intellectual‑Property Rights for Innovation  
- **Guarantee robust IP protections that enable youth‑driven innovation and entrepreneurship.**  
  *“Intellectual property rights were addressed as a means to empower youth innovation and entrepreneurship.”* [3]  

### 4. Participation in Policy‑Making  
- **Create formal mechanisms for youth to contribute to digital policymaking and governance at local, national, and international levels.**  
  *“Increase youth participation in digital policymaking and governance.”* [4]  

### 5. Protection of Children Online  
- **Uphold the dignity, security and privacy of children on the Internet, in line with the 86 Declaration of the Committee of Ministers.**  
  *“Declaration of the Committee of Ministers on protecting the dignity, security and privacy of children on the Internet.”* [5]  

### 6. Equitable & Secure Digital Future  
- **Insist that the digital future be equitable, secure and empowering for every young person, regardless of geography or socioeconomic status.**  
  *“Youth representatives called for ensuring the digital future is equitable, secure and empowering for all.”* [6]  

### 7. Recognition of Youth Voice  
- **Acknowledge the central importance of youth participation in shaping the digital ecosystem.**  
  *“Importance of youth participation in shaping digital future.”* [7]  

### 8. Active Involvement in Rights Advocacy  
- **Ensure children and young people are actively involved in rights‑advocacy and decision‑making processes concerning the digital environment.**  
  *“Children and young people should be actively involved in their rights advocacy and be included in decision‑making processes in the digital environment.”* [8]  

---

## Core Calls to Action  

1. **Establish Youth Seats** on multistakeholder bodies (e.g., IGF, UN cyber‑policy forums) to guarantee representation.  
2. **Fund Youth‑Led Digital Literacy & Disinformation‑Counter programmes**, especially in under‑served regions.  
3. **Legislate Youth‑Friendly IP frameworks** that lower barriers for young creators.  
4. **Mandate Youth Impact Assessments** for any new digital policy or technology deployment.  
5. **Adopt and enforce the 86 Declaration** as a binding standard for child‑online safety.  
6. **Allocate resources for equitable broadband access** and secure digital infrastructure for all youth.  
7. **Create mentorship pipelines** linking experienced policymakers with emerging youth leaders.  
8. **Integrate youth voices** into national cyber‑crime treaty negotiations and other emerging digital treaties.  

*By adopting these principles and actions, we commit to a digital world where the rights, dignity, and potential of every young person are fully respected and realized.*

</details>
