---

### Core Principles

1. **Dignity & Freedom** – Every young person has the right to an online environment that respects their humanity.  
2. **Inclusivity** – Policies must be shaped by a diversity of youth voices, especially those historically excluded.  
3. **Empowerment** – Access to tools, knowledge, and legal protections that enable young innovators to thrive.  
4. **Safety & Security** – Robust safeguards against abuse, surveillance, and cyber‑crime.  
5. **Transparency & Accountability** – Open reporting and genuine youth oversight of digital governance.

---

### Call to Action

- **Governments:** Ratify and operationalise the commitments above in national digital strategies.  
- **Private Sector:** Adopt youth‑friendly IP frameworks and invest in youth‑led innovation ecosystems.  
- **International Bodies (UN, IGF, etc.):** Institutionalise youth seats and fund capacity‑building programmes.  
- **Youth & Civil Society:** Organise, advocate, and monitor the implementation of this manifesto.

*By embracing these commitments, we can turn the promise of a digital future into a reality that is **equitable, secure, and empowering for all young people**.*

### Q65: core internet protocols, network security

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 5.33s | 5.08s (+0.28s expansion) |
| Score avg | 4.2/5 | 4.8/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 5 |
| **Winner** |  | **✓** |

> **Judge:** Both answers are thorough, but Answer A stays closer to likely source material and avoids questionable references, giving it higher accuracy and better source usage.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**Core Internet Protocols and Their Role in Network Security**

| Core Element | Why It Matters for Security | Normative / Technical Safeguards Mentioned in the Context |
|-------------|----------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------|
| **Internet Protocol (IP) & Routing (BGP)** | Provide the basic addressing and path‑selection mechanisms that keep data flowing across the global network. Compromise of these layers can enable hijacking, traffic interception, or denial‑of‑service attacks. | The public‑core norm treats these protocols as a *global public good* that must be shielded from “unwarranted state intervention” [4][5]. Robustness is reinforced by standards that protect the core infrastructure [3]. |
| **Domain Name System (DNS)** | Translates human‑readable names into IP addresses; a compromised DNS can redirect users to malicious sites. | **DNSSEC** (Domain Name System Security Extensions) adds cryptographic signatures to DNS data, ensuring authenticity and integrity. It is highlighted as a key mechanism for securing the core infrastructure [6][7]. |
| **Routing Security (RPKI)** | Validates that an IP prefix is legitimately announced by its rightful holder, preventing BGP route‑origin attacks. | **RPKI** (Resource Public Key Infrastructure) is repeatedly cited as essential for protecting the internet’s routing layer [6][7]. |
| **Internet Exchange Points (IXPs) & Cable Systems** | Physical and logical interconnection points that carry bulk traffic; attacks here can disrupt large swaths of the internet. | The public‑core framework explicitly includes IXPs and cable systems among the technical parameters that should be protected from weaponisation or state‑driven interference [8]. |

### How These Protocols Support the Core Security Goals

1. **Confidentiality, Integrity, Availability (CIA)** – The standards referenced (e.g., DNSSEC, RPKI) are designed to preserve the *integrity* of name resolution and routing data, thereby supporting overall *availability* of services and preventing unauthorized manipulation that could lead to confidentiality breaches [1][2].

2. **Re‑architecting the Internet Core** – By adopting and mandating these security‑focused standards, the internet can be “re‑architected” to be more resilient against cyber threats, as the IGF 2023 Open Forum notes [1][2].

3. **Normative Protection** – The “public core” concept, developed over a decade, frames core protocols and infrastructure as a **global public good**. This normative stance underpins legal and regulatory efforts to keep states from unjustified interference, thereby reinforcing technical safeguards with a legal‑policy layer [4][5].

### Take‑away for Diplomats and Policymakers

- **Promote Adoption of DNSSEC and RPKI**: Encourage member states and regional bodies to make these security extensions mandatory for critical national and private networks.
- **Embed the Public‑Core Norm in International Agreements**: Reference the public‑core principle in treaties or multilateral declarations to give it legal weight against state‑level cyber‑aggression.
- **Coordinate Across Sectors**: Align technical standards (IGF‑identified) with normative frameworks (public‑core) to create a cohesive defense‑in‑depth strategy for the internet’s backbone.

In short, the **core internet protocols**—IP, BGP, DNS, and the physical interconnection points—are the foundation of global connectivity. Their security is ensured through a blend of **technical standards** (DNSSEC, RPKI) and **normative frameworks** (the public‑core concept) that together aim to preserve the confidentiality, integrity, and availability of the internet in both peacetime and conflict scenarios [1][3][4][6][8].

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**Core Internet Protocols**

- The “core” of the Internet is made up of the fundamental protocols and infrastructure that keep data flowing worldwide – things like the IP addressing scheme, routing protocols (e.g., BGP), the Domain Name System (DNS), and the physical layers that include Internet exchange points and submarine cable systems [8].  
- These protocols are treated as a **global public good**; the “public‑core” concept explicitly frames them as essential technical parameters that should be shielded from unwarranted state interference [4][5].

**How Network Security Is Built Around Those Protocols**

| Security Mechanism | What It Secures | How It Relates to the Core |
|--------------------|----------------|----------------------------|
| **Standards & Technical Guidelines** | General confidentiality, integrity, and availability (CIA) of information across the whole Internet | By re‑architecting the Internet core with updated standards, the resilience of core protocols against cyber‑threats is improved [1][2] |
| **DNSSEC (Domain Name System Security Extensions)** | Authenticates DNS responses, preventing spoofing and cache‑poisoning attacks | Directly protects the DNS, one of the core protocols identified as essential for Internet function [6][7] |
| **RPKI (Resource Public Key Infrastructure)** | Validates the legitimacy of BGP route announcements, mitigating route‑hijacking | Secures the routing layer (BGP), another pillar of the core infrastructure [6][7] |
| **Normative Frameworks (Public‑Core Norm)** | Provides a legal‑policy shield that treats core protocols as exempt from unjustified state actions | Reinforces the technical safeguards by embedding them in international norms and legal protections [4][5] |
| **General Protocol & Standards Protection** | Maintains robustness of the underlying infrastructure | The IGF 2019 report notes that “protocols and standards protect the robustness of core Internet infrastructure” [3] |

**Key Take‑aways**

1. **Core protocols** (IP, DNS, BGP, exchange points, cables) are the backbone of global connectivity.  
2. **Network security** for these protocols is pursued on two fronts:  
   - **Technical controls** such as DNSSEC and RPKI that cryptographically verify the integrity of DNS data and routing announcements.  
   - **Normative/legal safeguards** (the public‑core norm) that recognize these protocols as a public good and limit state‑level interference, thereby supporting a stable and secure environment.  

Together, the combination of updated technical standards and internationally‑endorsed norms aims to keep the Internet’s core both **secure** and **resilient** against evolving cyber threats.

</details>

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**What the provided material tells us about ITU‑T Recommendations**

| Aspect | What the text says | Source |
|--------|-------------------|--------|
| **Quantity and status** | “Over 2600 ITU‑T Recommendations are currently in force.” The body of recommendations is continuously revised, and users are urged to check for the most recent editions. | 【3】 |
| **Publication practice** | A “list of the currently valid ITU‑T Recommendations is regularly published.” Each Recommendation includes references to other Recommendations that become part of its provisions. | 【1】, 【5】, 【7】 |
| **Examples of specific Recommendations** | • **Y.140 (2000)** – “Global Information Infrastructure (GII) Reference points for interconnection framework.” <br>• **Y.140.1 (2004)** – “Guideline for attributes and requirements for interconnection between public telecommunication network operators and service providers involved in provision of telecommunication services.” | 【1】 |
| **Series‑specific guidance** | • **X‑Series** – “It also makes suggestions that internet service providers might follow.” (general guidance for ISPs). <br>• **F‑Series** – Focuses on accessibility: “guidelines … for older persons and persons with disabilities … a good reference for producing any other Recommendations in ITU‑T to improve telecommunication accessibility.” | 【4】, 【6】 |
| **Scope of a Recommendation** | The text repeatedly notes that each Recommendation may incorporate other Recommendations by reference, and that the scope can be tailored (e.g., the F‑Series targeting accessibility). | 【7】 |
| **Legal notice** | The document is a formal ITU‑T Recommendation, subject to the ITU’s legal framework. | 【3】 |

### Key take‑aways

1. **ITU‑T Recommendations are technical standards** that define how telecommunication networks, services, and related technologies should operate globally.  
2. **They are numerous and evolving** – more than 2,600 are active, and each is periodically revised; users must verify they are consulting the latest edition.  
3. **They are organized into series** (e.g., Y‑Series for interconnection, X‑Series for ISP‑related guidance, F‑Series for accessibility).  
4. **Cross‑referencing is common**; a Recommendation may incorporate provisions from other Recommendations, creating an interconnected body of standards.  
5. **Official lists are maintained by the ITU** and published regularly, ensuring transparency about which Recommendations are currently valid.  

Thus, the context paints a picture of a large, structured, and continuously updated set of standards that the International Telecommunication Union (ITU‑T) uses to govern global telecommunication practices.

</details>

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**The vision for learning in India – what the speakers called “AI 2.0” – is built around four inter‑linked pillars**

| Pillar | What the speakers said | Why it matters |
|--------|-----------------------|----------------|
| **A long‑term, AI‑driven national ambition** | The goal is to re‑imagine education not for today but for “India of 2050, India of 2100” and to make the country the **number‑one global economy** by leveraging AI to break connectivity barriers and unlock the potential of its huge youth population【1†L1-L9】【4†L1-L4】. | Positions education as the engine of economic transformation and sets a clear, future‑oriented target for policy and investment. |
| **Scale of the current ecosystem** | Roughly **25 crore** children are in school and **4.6 crore** in higher education, spread across **15 lakh schools**. Only about **4.4 million** of those schools have computers, ICT labs or tablets – a massive infrastructure gap for taking AI to the “last mile”【2†L4-L9】【5†L1-L5】. | Highlights the sheer size of the challenge: millions of learners and schools must be equipped before AI can be mainstreamed. |
| **Human‑capital focus – AI‑savvy teachers** | India has about **1 crore teachers**, most of whom are women, but **few are AI‑literate**. Building AI competence among teachers is seen as essential, and the government is already working on it with the help of bodies such as the NCT (National Council of Teachers)【2†L9-L12】【5†L6-L9】. | Teachers are the conduit for any curriculum change; their AI literacy determines how effectively students will adopt new tools and mind‑sets. |
| **Curricular and pedagogical shift** | A **computational‑thinking and AI curriculum** is planned to start **from Grade 3**, with rollout expected in the next academic season【8†L1-L4】. The broader message is that success will be measured not by the number of AI tools in classrooms but by how well the youth are prepared to lead the AI era【7†L1-L4】. | Early exposure builds a pipeline of future innovators and ensures that AI becomes a foundational skill rather than a niche add‑on. |

### How these pieces fit together – a picture of the future

1. **Infrastructure expansion** – Massive investment will be needed to equip the 15 lakh schools (especially the 4.4 million that already have some ICT) with reliable internet, AI‑ready hardware, and maintenance support so that even remote villages can “speak in Bhojpuri to the US, Russia, Japan” as the speaker imagined【1†L9-L12】.

2. **Teacher up‑skilling programmes** – Large‑scale professional‑development initiatives will be launched to turn the 1 crore teachers into AI‑literate educators, leveraging partnerships with industry and research institutions.

3. **Curriculum redesign** – From Grade 3 onward, computational thinking and AI concepts will be woven into the syllabus, moving away from rote learning toward problem‑solving, data literacy, and ethical AI awareness.

4. **Integrated higher‑education strategy** – The same AI‑focused mindset will be applied to the 4.6 crore higher‑education students, aligning university programmes with industry needs and research priorities to keep pace with global AI advances.

5. **Economic and societal impact** – By 2050‑2100, the expectation is that an AI‑enabled education system will have turned India into a **leading global economy**, with a workforce capable of creating, governing, and ethically deploying AI technologies worldwide【1†L13-L16】【4†L1-L4】.

### Key challenges to watch

- **Closing the digital divide** – Scaling hardware, connectivity, and maintenance to the last‑mile schools remains a “huge challenge”【5†L1-L5】.  
- **Ensuring gender equity in AI literacy** – While most teachers are women, their AI competence must be raised to match the ambition.  
- **Sustaining political and financial commitment** – The speakers repeatedly stress the need for “your help” and coordinated government action to keep the AI boom from being left behind【2†L10-L12】.

### Bottom line

The future of learning in India, as outlined in the AI 2.0 discussions, envisions an **AI‑infused, universally accessible education system** that starts early (Grade 3), equips teachers, upgrades school infrastructure, and ultimately fuels a transformation of India into a **global AI leader and top‑ranked economy by mid‑century**. The roadmap is ambitious, and its success hinges on bridging the current infrastructure gap, scaling teacher AI literacy, and delivering a curriculum that prepares youth to **lead**—not just use—AI.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**Future of Learning in India – Key Themes from the Provided Sources**

| Aspect | What the Vision Looks Like | Evidence |
|--------|----------------------------|----------|
| **Long‑term horizon** | The discussion is not about today’s system but about India in **2050‑2100**, when the country aspires to be a **number‑one global economy** driven by AI‑enabled education. | [1], [4] |
| **AI as the catalyst** | AI is seen as the technology that will “dismantle the barrier” of connectivity, allowing anyone—even a villager speaking Bhojpuri—to communicate globally and participate in the knowledge economy. | [1] |
| **Scale of the challenge** | • **≈25 crore** children in school and **≈4.6 crore** in higher education today.<br>• **≈15 lakh** schools exist, but only **≈4.4 million** have computers, ICT labs, tablets, etc.<br>• **≈1 crore** teachers (mostly women) are in the system, yet few are AI‑savvy. | [2], [5] |
| **Infrastructure & last‑mile reach** | The biggest hurdle is extending AI‑enabled tools to the **last mile**—the millions of schools that still lack basic digital infrastructure. | [2], [5] |
| **Teacher capacity building** | A concerted effort is underway to make teachers AI‑literate, recognizing that they are the frontline for any curriculum change. | [2], [5] |
| **Curriculum reform** | The government plans to **introduce computational thinking and AI concepts from Grade 3**, with rollout expected in the next academic season. | [8] |
| **Integrated approach** | Stakeholders (government, academia, industry) are working together on an “integrated approach” that links school‑level reforms with higher‑education transformation. | [2] |
| **Economic ambition** | The ultimate goal is to convert the educational transformation into **economic growth**, positioning India alongside (or ahead of) China as a leading AI‑driven economy. | [1], [4] |
| **Measuring success** | Success will be judged not by the number of AI tools deployed, but by how well the youth are **prepared to lead** the nation in the AI era. | [7] |

### Summary Narrative

The future of learning in India is envisioned as an **AI‑centric, inclusive system** that prepares a massive youth cohort for a knowledge‑based economy by mid‑century. The plan hinges on three pillars:

1. **Infrastructure Expansion** – Scaling digital labs, computers, and connectivity to the **4.4 million** schools that currently lack them, thereby reaching the “last mile.”
2. **Human‑Capital Development** – Upskilling the **≈1 crore** teachers (predominantly women) to become AI‑literate, ensuring they can deliver the new curriculum.
3. **Curricular Innovation** – Embedding **computational thinking and AI** from **Grade 3** onward, creating a pipeline of students who can fluently engage with AI technologies.

If these pillars are realized, India aims to shift from being a large consumer of AI to a **global leader** in AI‑driven economic growth, fulfilling the ambition of becoming the **number‑one economy** by 2050‑2100. The transformation is framed not merely as technology adoption but as a **societal shift** that empowers every learner—from a village AI lab to the world stage.

</details>

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

AI safety was a recurring theme across the various forums and high‑level meetings cited in the material.  Participants highlighted several concrete concerns and proposed approaches:

* **Broad‑based debate on safety** – The keynote interview notes that “a bunch of good forums around the world [are] debating AI safety issues” [1].

* **Deceptive behavior and advanced‑AI risks** – In the “Future of Artificial General Intelligence” report, leaders warned that future systems could act deceptively and that the overall risk profile of advanced AI must be managed [3].

* **Military and existential hazards** – The UN Security Council’s 9821st meeting focused on “implementing robust safeguards against the risks posed by AI in military applications,” discussed “accidental risks associated with AI‑enabled systems,” and flagged “existential risks posed by AI” [4].

* **Responsible, safe AI for all** – Speakers at the AI Summit—including heads of state—emphasised the need for “responsible, safe AI and AI for everyone” [5][8].

* **Governance, safeguards and ethical frameworks** – The Leaders’ Plenary session listed “AI Safety, Security and Ethics” as a priority, calling for proper safeguards, cybersecurity measures and ethical frameworks when developing AI [7].

* **Guidelines and standards to mitigate technical threats** – The cybersecurity‑focused WS #31 discussions highlighted the need for “guidelines and standards to help organizations implement AI securely,” addressing issues such as data poisoning, model security and supply‑chain vulnerabilities, which are integral to overall AI safety [2][6].

In sum, the discussions covered the spectrum of AI safety—from technical threats like deception and data‑poisoning to strategic dangers in military use and existential risk—while urging the creation of robust safeguards, ethical governance structures, and international cooperation to ensure AI is developed and deployed responsibly.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

The various sessions and reports repeatedly highlighted that **AI safety** was a central theme of the meetings.  Participants talked about:

* **Broad‑scale safety and risk‑management concerns** – leaders warned that advanced systems can behave deceptively and pose “broader risks” to society, including accidental or even existential threats, especially when AI is embedded in military‑type applications [3][4].

* **Robust safeguards and responsible development** – the UN Security Council focused on “implementing robust safeguards” against AI‑enabled risks, while the Global Vision plenary stressed the need for “proper safeguards, cybersecurity measures, and ethical frameworks” to develop AI responsibly [4][7].

* **Ethical and governance frameworks** – speakers from the AI Summit (e.g., Prime Minister Modi, President Macron) and the IGF‑2024 workshop called for “responsible, safe AI” and stressed that AI should be “for everyone” under clear governance [5][8].

* **Cyber‑security‑related safety measures** – the IGF‑2024 workshop “WS #31 Cybersecurity in AI” examined trust, transparency, and the need for guidelines and standards to protect AI systems from data‑poisoning, model‑security flaws, and supply‑chain vulnerabilities [2][6].

* **Education and literacy** – the same workshop highlighted the importance of AI literacy and education as part of a broader safety strategy [2].

In short, the discussions covered **technical risks (deceptive behavior, data‑poisoning, model security), strategic risks (accidental or existential threats, especially in military use), and governance/ethical measures (safeguards, standards, responsible development, and public education)** to ensure AI is deployed safely. [1][3][4][5][7][2][6]

### Q72: what is ecmascript?

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 1.97s | 2.51s (+0.26s expansion) |
| Score avg | 4.5/5 | 4.8/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 2 |
| **Winner** |  | **✓** |

> **Judge:** Answer A provides a more complete and detailed overview (including history and committee details) while both are accurate; B has slightly better citation granularity but overall A is stronger.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

ECMAScript is the **general‑purpose, cross‑platform, vendor‑neutral programming language** that underlies JavaScript (Netscape) and JScript (Microsoft).  It is the language specified and maintained by **Ecma International** (ECMA‑262) and is the core scripting language of the Web browser application platform, while also being used in many other environments such as servers, mobile devices and even resource‑constrained embedded systems (micro‑controllers, single‑board computers)【2†L1-L4】【3†L1-L5】.  

The language is defined in the **ECMAScript Language Specification** (ECMA‑262) and related standards (e.g., the Internationalization API, ECMA‑402) published by Ecma International【6†L4-L8】.  Because it is an open, standardized language, it can be implemented by any vendor and has “several billion implementations” worldwide【4†L1-L2】.  

In short, ECMAScript is the standardized programming language that powers JavaScript on the web and is increasingly used beyond the browser in diverse computing contexts.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

ECMAScript is the **general‑purpose, cross‑platform, vendor‑neutral programming language** that underlies JavaScript (originally from Netscape) and JScript (Microsoft).  It is the language defined and maintained by **Ecma International** through the ECMA‑262 specification and related standards [2][4][6].  

Key points from the supplied material:

* **Scope and purpose** – ECMAScript is the programming language of the Web browser application platform, but it is also used in many other environments, from servers and mobile devices to resource‑constrained embedded systems such as micro‑controllers and single‑board computers [3][6].  
* **Standardization** – The language is standardized by Ecma International (TC 39) and is listed among the organization’s official technical committees (TC 39 for ECMAScript and TC 53 for ECMAScript modules for embedded systems) [7].  
* **Historical background** – The first edition was published in 1997; since then it has grown to become one of the world’s most widely used general‑purpose languages, with “several billion implementations” worldwide [2][4].  

In short, **ECMAScript is the standardized language specification that defines the behavior of JavaScript and related implementations, providing a stable, compatible foundation for web and non‑web software development** [2][3][4][6].

</details>

**Multistakeholder dialogue on Internet governance** is a collaborative, inclusive process that brings together a broad spectrum of actors — governments, civil‑society groups, the private sector, technical communities, and academia — to discuss, shape, and influence policies and practices that affect the global Internet.

Key points drawn from the provided context:

   - Sessions such as “Main Session 4: Looking back, moving forward – how to continue to empower the IGF’s role in Internet Governance” and the “OPEN DIALOGUE WITH IGF LEADERSHIP PANEL” explicitly focus on the multistakeholder model【2†L1-L3】【8†L1-L3】.

2. **Broad participation across sectors**  
   - Leaders and advocates repeatedly emphasize that the model’s strength lies in its “inclusiveness and capacity to draw on insights from diverse sectors, including government, civil society, the private sector, technical communities, and academia”【3†L1-L3】.  
   - The “Informal Stakeholder Consultation Session” reinforces this by calling for a multistakeholder approach that “encompasses all stakeholders, civil society, academia, the technical community, and the private sector”【7†L1-L3】.

3. **Regional and thematic extensions**  
   - The IGF’s LAC (Latin America and Caribbean) Space also centers on “Internet Governance, Multi‑stakeholder dialogue, ICANN,” showing that the model is applied in regional contexts as well【4†L1-L3】.  
   - Various forums (e.g., Open Forum #78 “Shaping the Future with Multistakeholder Foresight” and Open Forum #18 “World Economic Forum – Building Trustworthy Governance”) discuss the multistakeholder approach as a way to address the complex, evolving challenges of Internet governance【5†L1-L2】【6†L1-L2】.

4. **Rationale for the multistakeholder model**  
   - Advocates argue that this model is essential for “trustworthy governance” and for ensuring that policy decisions reflect the needs and expertise of all parts of the Internet ecosystem【6†L1-L2】.  
   - Its inclusive nature is seen as a way to harness diverse insights, making governance more resilient, adaptable, and legitimate.

**In summary**, multistakeholder dialogue on Internet governance is an ongoing, globally recognized practice—anchored by the IGF and reinforced through numerous regional and thematic sessions—that seeks to involve every relevant constituency in shaping the rules, norms, and technical standards that underpin the Internet. This approach is valued for its inclusiveness, diversity of expertise, and capacity to produce balanced, trustworthy outcomes.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

Multistakeholder dialogue is the cornerstone of contemporary Internet governance.  It brings together **government representatives, civil‑society groups, the private sector, technical communities, and academia** in a single, open forum where each can share perspectives, expertise, and policy ideas.  

Key points drawn from the provided sources:

1. **The Internet Governance Forum (IGF) is explicitly designed as a multistakeholder dialogue space** – it “is a space for dialogue, involving different stakeholders (multistakeholder)” [1].  

2. **The IGF’s ongoing conversations cover a wide range of internet‑related topics** within this inclusive setting, reinforcing the model’s breadth and relevance [2].  

3. **Leaders and experts repeatedly cite the model’s inclusiveness and its ability to draw on diverse insights** from government, civil society, the private sector, technical communities, and academia [3], [7].  

4. **Regional and thematic IGF events (e.g., IGF LAC Space, Open Forum #78, Open Forum #18, and the “Open Dialogue with IGF Leadership Panel”) continue to use the multistakeholder format** to discuss governance challenges, trust‑building, and future foresight [4]‑[8].  

In practice, multistakeholder dialogue means that no single group dominates the decision‑making process; instead, policies and recommendations emerge from collaborative discussion, ensuring that the Internet’s technical, economic, social, and political dimensions are all considered. This approach is seen as essential for “empowering the IGF’s role in Internet Governance” and for shaping trustworthy, forward‑looking governance frameworks [2], [6].

</details>

| Agreement (as cited) | Parties | Main purpose / key provisions highlighted in the text |
|-----------------------|---------|--------------------------------------------------------|
| **Free Trade Agreement between the EFTA States and the Republic of Serbia** | European Free Trade Association (Iceland, Liechtenstein, Norway, Switzerland) + Serbia | • Creates “favourable conditions for the development and diversification of trade” and promotes commercial/economic co‑operation on the basis of equality, non‑discrimination and international law. <br>• Re‑affirms obligations under multilateral environmental agreements and fundamental workers’ rights (ILO conventions). <br>• Calls for simplification of trade procedures, cooperation on trade‑facilitation, and work of a Joint Committee. <br>• Includes commitments to respect ILO fundamental principles (freedom of association, abolition of forced/child labour, non‑discrimination). <br>• References a range of international environmental and social declarations (Stockholm, Rio, Agenda 21, Johannesburg, Rio+20, ILO declarations, UN‑ESC 2006, ILO 2008). <br>• Provides for exchange of information, prompt responses to queries, and mutual understanding of government‑procurement rules with a view to liberalisation. | [1]–[5], [8] |
| **Free Trade Agreement between the Republic of Albania and Serbia and Montenegro** | Albania + Serbia + Montenegro | • Parties examine possibilities for additional concessions. <br>• Grants each other specific concessions (see “Protocol A”) to facilitate trade in agricultural products, in line with the chapter’s provisions. | [6] |
| **Stabilisation and Association Agreement (SAA) – Serbia and the European Union** | Serbia + EU (European Commission) | • Serbia must supply the EU Commission with details of its trade agreements (dates of entry into force, rules of origin) that it applies with other countries/territories listed in paragraph 1 of the SAA. | [7] |

### How these agreements relate to Serbia

1. **EFTA‑Serbia FTA** – Gives Serbia preferential market access to the four EFTA economies and obliges both sides to uphold high standards on labour rights, environment and trade‑facilitation.  
2. **Albania‑Serbia‑Montenegro FTA** – A regional arrangement focused on agricultural trade, allowing the three parties to deepen market integration in that sector.  
3. **Stabilisation and Association Agreement** – The EU’s primary framework for Serbia’s political and economic alignment with the Union; it requires transparency about Serbia’s other trade deals (including the two FTAs above).

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

The Internet Engineering Task Force (IETF) is an **open, international community** made up of network designers, operators, vendors, and researchers. Its purpose is to develop and promote **voluntary Internet standards**, especially those that form the Internet protocol suite. The IETF produces technical and engineering documents—including protocol standards, best‑current‑practice guides, and informational notes—through the **Requests for Comments (RFC)** series. Its mission is to create documents that help the Internet work better and evolve smoothly [3][6].

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

The **Internet Engineering Task Force (IETF)** is an open, international community of network designers, operators, vendors, and researchers that develops and promotes voluntary technical standards for the Internet.  It produces the Requests for Comments (RFC) series, which include protocol standards, best‑current‑practice guidelines, and informational documents that help the Internet architecture evolve and operate smoothly.  Its core mission is to create technical and engineering documents that make the Internet work better — from core protocol specifications to practical operational recommendations [3][6].

</details>

### Q79: What was discussed about AI safety at the global level?

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|

| Score avg | 4.0/5 | 5.0/5 |

> **Judge:** Answer A covers all key themes, adds the specific report detail, and provides precise source citations, making it more complete, specific, and well‑referenced.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

The global‑level dialogue on AI safety centred on a handful of core, widely‑shared conclusions:

* **International cooperation beats isolation** – Ministers and experts agreed that the biggest safety challenges cannot be solved by any single country acting alone; they called for partnership‑based frameworks rather than “AI‑sovereignty”‑first policies [4][5][7].

* **Scientific rigor in AI evaluation** – Participants stressed that safety assessments must be grounded in robust, peer‑reviewed science and transparent benchmarking, not ad‑hoc or politically‑driven checks [4].

* **Focus on multi‑agent system risks** – The panel highlighted that the emergence of AI systems that interact with one another (e.g., large language models, autonomous agents) creates novel failure modes that require dedicated research and coordinated safeguards [4].

* **Address both immediate and systemic risks** – Delegates said policy must tackle short‑term hazards (e.g., misuse, bias, reliability) while also planning for longer‑term, systemic threats such as runaway optimization or coordination failures [4].

* **Balancing national AI sovereignty with global standards** – While some nations pursue “AI sovereignty” to protect strategic interests, the consensus was that a collaborative, standards‑driven approach is essential to avoid a fragmented safety landscape [5][7].

In short, the global discussion underscored that AI safety is a shared, scientific‑driven endeavour that requires coordinated, partnership‑oriented policies to manage both current and future risks. [4][5][7]

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

The global‑level dialogue on AI safety focused on four main themes that were repeatedly highlighted by the digital‑minister panels and the AI Safety Connect summit in New Delhi:

1. **International cooperation over isolation** – Participants stressed that the biggest safety challenges cannot be solved by any single nation acting alone.  They argued for partnership‑based frameworks rather than “AI‑sovereignty” approaches that would fragment standards and impede collective risk mitigation【5†L1-L4】【7†L1-L4】.  

2. **Scientific rigor in AI evaluation** – A consensus emerged that robust, peer‑reviewed methodologies are essential for assessing AI systems, especially as they become more complex.  This includes transparent benchmarking, reproducible testing, and the use of independent audit mechanisms【4†L1-L3】.  

3. **Prioritising multi‑agent system risks** – The ministers identified the emergence of interacting AI agents (e.g., large‑language‑model ecosystems, autonomous bots) as a high‑impact risk vector that requires dedicated research, monitoring, and governance structures【4†L1-L3】.  

4. **Addressing both immediate and systemic risks** – The discussion differentiated short‑term hazards (such as misuse, hallucinations, or deployment‑time failures) from longer‑term, systemic threats (including capability amplification, strategic instability, and alignment loss).  Both categories were deemed urgent and needed coordinated policy responses【4†L1-L3】.  

These points were framed around the release of a **global AI safety report** chaired by Yoshua Bengio, which synthesized technical findings and policy recommendations for governments and international bodies【8†L1-L2】.  The overall message was that a coordinated, scientifically grounded, and partnership‑oriented approach is essential to manage the evolving risks of AI at the global scale.

</details>

**Norway’s post‑COVID‑19 policy focus (as outlined in the supplied documents)**  

| Area | Main actions / priorities | Source |
|------|---------------------------|--------|
| **Economic restructuring** | • End the coronavirus‑related measures that hinder the restructuring of the Norwegian economy as soon as it is prudent to do so. <br>• Pursue stronger competitiveness, reduced greenhouse‑gas emissions, green growth, a well‑functioning labour market, high employment and overall economic sustainability. | [1] |
| **Digital transformation & public services** | • Use competence‑enhancement and digital‑public‑service initiatives that were introduced during the crisis to leave Norway in a stronger position after the pandemic. <br>• Revise regulations to remove barriers and ensure clarity and consistency across agencies (One Digital Public Sector strategy 2019‑2025). | [4]; [6] |
| **Living digital strategy for development aid** | • Keep the digital strategy for Norwegian development policy “alive” – continuously adjust it as new knowledge and rapid digitalisation developments emerge. <br>• Learn from Norway’s own projects, from international partners, and from feedback from business and civil society. | [2] |
| **Multilateral digitalisation** | • Promote digitalisation within the governing bodies of key multilateral organisations (World Bank, WFP, UNICEF, WHO, UNDP). <br>• Emphasise knowledge transfer and strategic partnerships with these bodies, maintaining and intensifying digital transformation of Norwegian development assistance. | [3]; [7] |
| **Humanitarian & development digital innovation** | • Launch a digital innovation programme as part of the new humanitarian policy strategy. <br>• Share digital knowledge and experience with partner countries through a “Digitalisation for Development” programme that builds skills and capacity. <br>• Intensify support for local incubators and innovation centres, both directly (through existing strategic partnerships) and indirectly via the World Bank’s infoDev programme to promote entrepreneurship. | [5] |
| **Sector‑specific focus** | • Continue to prioritise the ocean and small‑island developing states (as set out in the Government’s Oceans Strategy) while applying the above digital and economic measures. | [7] |

**In short:** Norway’s post‑COVID agenda combines (1) the timely removal of pandemic‑related restrictions that impede economic restructuring, (2) a push for greener, more competitive growth and a robust labour market, (3) a sustained, adaptive digital strategy that upgrades public services and development aid, (4) deeper digital cooperation with multilateral organisations, and (5) targeted digital‑innovation programmes for humanitarian and development partners. All of these measures are intended to both address the immediate fallout of the pandemic and to position Norway for stronger, more resilient growth thereafter.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**Norway’s post‑COVID‑19 policy framework combines three main strands:**

| Area | Key actions | Source |
|------|-------------|--------|
| **Economic & labour‑market recovery** | • End the pandemic‑related measures that have limited the ability to restructure the economy as soon as it is prudent.<br>• Pursue stronger competitiveness, lower greenhouse‑gas emissions, green growth, a well‑functioning labour market, high employment and overall economic sustainability. | 【1】 |
| **Digital transformation & public‑service modernization** | • Accelerate competence‑enhancement programmes and the development of digital public services – measures that both help manage the crisis and leave Norway in a stronger position afterward.<br>• Treat the digital strategy for development policy as a “living” document that will be continuously adjusted in line with new knowledge and rapid digitalisation trends.<br>• Revise regulations to remove barriers and create clearer, more consistent rules across agencies (the “One Digital Public Sector” strategy). | 【4】, 【2】, 【6】 |
| **International development & innovation** | • Launch a Digital Innovation Programme as part of the new humanitarian‑policy strategy.<br>• Share digital know‑how with partner countries through a “Digitalisation for Development” programme that builds skills and capacity.<br>• Boost support for local incubators and innovation centres, both directly via strategic partnerships and indirectly through the World Bank’s infoDev programme. | 【5】 |

**In short, Norway’s post‑COVID‑19 agenda is to:**

1. **Phase out pandemic‑specific restrictions** once it is safe, while using the recovery period to reinforce competitiveness, green growth and labour‑market resilience.  
2. **Fast‑track digitalisation** across the public sector and development aid, improving digital services, removing regulatory obstacles and continuously updating the digital strategy.  
3. **Leverage digital innovation internationally**, helping partner countries build digital capacity and fostering entrepreneurship through incubators and multilateral programmes.  

These combined measures are intended to both address the immediate fallout from the pandemic and position Norway for long‑term, sustainable growth.

### Q82: What are key disagreements in the UN Cybercrime Convention?

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 5.35s | 5.07s (+0.94s expansion) |
| Score avg | 4.8/5 | 4.0/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 7 |
| **Winner** | **✓** |  |

> **Judge:** Answer A covers a broader set of disagreement points and gives more concrete details, while still staying accurate and citing sources effectively.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

The negotiations have stalled around a handful of “deal‑breaker” issues that keep re‑emerging in every UN session:

| Area of disagreement | What the states cannot agree on | Why it matters |
|----------------------|--------------------------------|----------------|
| **Scope of the treaty** | How broadly “cybercrime” and “serious crimes” are defined and whether the convention should cover **any offense that a domestic law labels as cyber‑related** or only a narrow set of internationally‑recognised crimes. The draft leaves the extent of cross‑border surveillance cooperation vague, creating the risk that states could invoke the treaty to justify sweeping data grabs that are not uniformly defined elsewhere. | An overly‑broad scope would produce **inconsistent application** across jurisdictions, because national legal definitions differ, and could dilute existing standards for what counts as a serious cyber offence. [2] |
| **Human‑rights safeguards** | Whether the treaty must embed **mandatory protections** such as prior judicial authorisation, transparency of data requests, and strong guarantees against arbitrary interference with privacy and freedom of expression. Some delegations want these safeguards written into the text; others prefer to leave them to **national law**. | Without explicit safeguards, the convention could permit **real‑time data collection or communication interception** with little oversight, undermining the human‑rights framework that the EFF and other civil‑society groups consider essential. [1] |
| **Intrusive powers / data collection** | The legality of **real‑time data collection, bulk data retention, and cross‑border interception** without strong, enforceable human‑rights protections. | Such powers could be used for mass surveillance, raising concerns that the treaty would lower global privacy standards. [1] |
| **International‑cooperation mechanisms** | The exact **procedures for mutual legal assistance, evidence sharing, and cross‑border enforcement**. This section is contested because some states want streamlined, rapid cooperation; others demand stricter procedural safeguards to protect their citizens’ data. | The lack of consensus threatens the treaty’s **operational effectiveness** and could create a fragmented regime that conflicts with existing cooperation frameworks. [5] |
| **Compatibility with existing instruments** | Whether the new UN treaty can coexist with the **Budapest Convention** and other regional agreements, or whether it will **override or undermine** those standards. | If the UN text is incompatible, it could create a **dual‑track system** where states must navigate conflicting obligations, weakening overall cyber‑crime governance. [4] |
| **Adequate privacy safeguards** | The presence (or absence) of **uniform privacy protections** across signatories. Some delegations argue for a baseline of rights; others rely on domestic legislation, leading to **disparities**. | Disparate privacy regimes could result in “privacy dumping,” where data is routed through jurisdictions with weaker safeguards. [6] |

In short, the core disagreements revolve around **how far the treaty’s reach should extend (scope), what human‑rights and privacy guarantees must be built in (safeguards and intrusive powers), how states will cooperate across borders, and whether the new instrument can sit comfortably alongside the Budapest Convention and other existing frameworks**. Until these points are resolved, a final, globally‑binding UN Cybercrime Convention remains out of reach. [1][2][4][5][6]

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

The negotiations on a new UN Cybercrime Convention have repeatedly stalled around a handful of “red‑line” issues that separate the positions of states.  The most frequently‑cited points of contention are:

| Area of disagreement | What the dispute concerns | Why it matters |
|----------------------|---------------------------|----------------|
| **Scope of cross‑border surveillance and data‑collection powers** | States cannot agree on how broadly the treaty should permit real‑time data collection, traffic‑flow analysis or interception of communications for any offence that a country classifies as cyber‑crime.  The draft language leaves the “serious crimes” threshold vague, which could allow expansive, cross‑border surveillance without clear limits. | Without a narrow, well‑defined scope, the treaty could legitimize mass‑surveillance practices that exceed what is currently allowed under the Budapest Convention and other human‑rights instruments. |
| **Human‑rights safeguards (judicial authorisation, transparency, proportionality)** | Delegates argue over whether the treaty must embed mandatory safeguards such as prior judicial authorisation for data access, transparent reporting, and strong proportionality tests.  Critics (e.g., Katitza Rodriguez of the EFF) stress that these protections are currently left to national law rather than being enshrined internationally. | The absence of binding safeguards raises the risk that the treaty would erode privacy and other fundamental rights, creating “disparities in privacy protections across different countries.” |
| **International‑cooperation mechanisms** | The chapter on cooperation (mutual legal assistance, evidence‑sharing, and cross‑border investigations) is contested.  Some states want broader, faster channels for data exchange; others fear such mechanisms could bypass domestic safeguards and enable “intrusive powers” without oversight. | This tension mirrors broader worries that the UN treaty could be “incompatible with existing instruments” and could weaken the human‑rights framework that underpins the Budapest Convention. |
| **Compatibility with existing instruments** | A recurring concern is whether the new treaty would clash with the Council of Europe’s Budapest Convention and other regional regimes that already contain detailed privacy and due‑process guarantees. | If the UN text diverges, it could create a fragmented global landscape where standards vary widely, potentially “lead[ing] to a decline in global standards for human rights and privacy protection.” |
| **Definition of “serious crimes” and the breadth of offences covered** | The draft treats any offence defined under domestic law as potentially subject to the treaty’s data‑collection powers, creating uncertainty about which conduct qualifies as a “serious cybercrime.” | This ambiguity could result in inconsistent application across jurisdictions and enable states to invoke the treaty for relatively minor offences. |

These disagreements—particularly the lack of consensus on surveillance scope, mandatory human‑rights safeguards, and the mechanics of international cooperation—are why earlier UN sessions have failed to produce a final, binding convention on cybercrime【1†L1-L5】【2†L1-L4】【5†L1-L2】.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**Social impacts**

* **Public‑service transformation and everyday life** – The WSIS+20 Resolution notes that digital technologies have “transformed the ways in which individuals and communities interact, consume and spend their time” and have generated both positive outcomes and concerns for human rights, education, health, employment and overall welfare【1】.  
* **Conflict‑related disruption** – The United Nations Security Council highlights that “the human as well as the social and economic impact of conflict are massive,” underscoring how violence can fracture societies and erode social cohesion【2】.  
* **Road‑safety and mobility** – AI‑driven transport systems create “significant social implications, with productive population segments disproportionately affected by road accidents,” indicating that new mobility solutions can reshape risk distribution across society【6】.  
* **Digital platform proliferation** – A policy brief points out that the “growing use of digital platforms has economic, social and political impacts,” suggesting shifts in how people communicate, organize, and access services【7】.  
* **Inequality and governance** – The Global Risks Report 2020 warns that “the profound political consequences of inequality can also undermine economic growth by making a country harder to govern,” linking social disparity to political instability and reduced public trust【8】.  

**Economic impacts**

* **Service delivery and productivity** – The WSIS+20 Resolution emphasizes that digital change “has had profound impacts on the provision of public services,” which can boost efficiency but also raise cost and access concerns for sectors such as health and education【1】.  
* **Broad‑scope technical effects** – OECD digital‑economy papers stress that “any category of technical impact can have various economic and social consequences,” indicating that emerging technologies can ripple through markets, labor demand and value chains【3】【4】.  
* **Sector‑wide transformation** – The opening introduction notes that the impact is “profoundly social, political, economical,” framing digital change as a driver of macro‑economic shifts across industries【5】.  
* **Platform‑driven market changes** – The same policy brief highlights that digital platforms reshape competition, create new business models, and affect employment patterns, thereby altering economic structures【7】.  
* **Inequality‑driven growth drag** – According to the Global Risks Report, when inequality leads to political paralysis, it can “undermine economic growth,” showing a direct link between social disparity and reduced GDP performance【8】.  

**Overall picture**

Across the cited sources, the consensus is that digital transformation and related technological advances produce **interlinked social and economic effects**:

* They reshape how people interact, access services, and stay safe (social).  
* They alter productivity, market dynamics, public‑service costs, and growth trajectories (economic).  
* Negative side‑effects—such as heightened inequality, conflict‑induced disruption, or uneven safety outcomes—can amplify both social strain and economic slowdown.  

These observations collectively illustrate that any technical or policy decision in the digital realm must consider its **dual social‑economic ramifications** to maximize benefits and mitigate harms.

</details>

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

The **Internet Governance Forum (IGF)** is a **multistakeholder policy‑dialogue platform** created under the Tunis Agenda of the World Summit on the Information Society (WSIS) (Paragraph 72). Its purpose is to bring together governments, the private sector, civil society, the technical community and academia to discuss public‑policy issues that affect the core elements of Internet governance — such as access, security, diversity and openness — and to seek practical solutions rather than to make binding decisions.  

Key functions highlighted in the provided material include:

* **Open discussion of public‑policy issues** related to the Internet’s key elements, as defined in the Tunis Agenda [1][2].  
* **Focus on everyday user concerns**, including the use and misuse of the Internet, and on critical Internet resources [3].  
* **Four thematic areas** for its meetings: **access, security, diversity, and openness** [3].  
* **Strengthening stakeholder engagement**, especially from developing countries, in existing and future Internet‑governance mechanisms [3].  
* The IGF is described as a **platform for open discussions to identify solutions rather than make decisions** [8].

Thus, the Internet Governance Forum serves as a global, inclusive venue where a wide range of actors can dialogue on how the Internet should be governed, aiming to shape policies that are equitable, secure, and open for all.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

The **Internet Governance Forum (IGF)** is the multistakeholder platform created under Paragraph 72 of the Tunis Agenda of the World Summit on the Information Society (WSIS) to serve as a forum for policy dialogue on the public‑policy issues that shape the Internet.  It brings together governments, the private sector, civil society, the technical community and academia to discuss “key elements of Internet governance” — including access, security, diversity and openness — and to help find solutions to the challenges that arise from the use and misuse of the Internet, especially for everyday users and developing‑country stakeholders [1][2][3][8].  

Key characteristics of the IGF:

* **Open, multistakeholder dialogue** – it does not make binding decisions but aims to identify solutions through inclusive discussion [8].  
* **Broad thematic coverage** – sessions address critical Internet resources, security, diversity, openness, and the engagement of developing‑country participants [3].  
* **Regular convenings** – the forum is held annually (e.g., IGF 2014, IGF 2023, IGF 2024) with various thematic and side events such as “Internet Governance and Sovereignty” and “Legalization of data governance” [4][5][7][8].  

Thus, the Internet Governance Forum is the principal global venue for multistakeholder exchange on Internet governance issues.

</details>

It encompasses the way the diplomatic environment is changing, the adoption of online tools for communication, negotiation, virtual meetings, public‑diplomacy outreach and cultural exchange, and the emergence of new agenda items such as privacy, data protection and cybersecurity [2][4][5].  In short, it is the interplay between digital technology and the practice of diplomacy, where the internet becomes a core instrument for representation, negotiation and the delivery of diplomatic services [3][7][8].

> **Judge:** Both answers are comparable, but B offers a more concrete example (the 1963 Nuclear Test Ban Treaty) and thus scores higher on specificity, giving it the edge.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**What “test diplomacy” looks like in the material you provided**

| Aspect of “testing” | How it appears in diplomatic practice | Source |
|--------------------|--------------------------------------|--------|
| **Treaties that began as “tests” of cooperation** | Early nuclear‑test‑ban treaties were the product of bilateral Soviet‑American negotiations that served as a first, limited experiment in arms‑control dialogue before broader multilateral frameworks were built. | [1], [2], [3] |
| **Diplomacy treated as a “beta version”** | Modern diplomacy is described as a functional but still‑evolving system –‑ “beta” –‑ where rules, institutions and norms are continuously tested, adapted and iterated in real time as new technologies (e.g., algorithms) are deployed. | [4] |
| **Low‑pressure environments for trying out ideas** | Security‑diplomacy settings deliberately create low‑pressure spaces (workshops, informal meetings) that allow participants to test new concepts and build personal relationships without the stakes of formal negotiations. | [6] |
| **Workshops and training as test grounds** | Workshops such as those at the Internet Governance Forum (IGF) are explicitly framed as “about diplomacy” and function as experimental venues where diplomatic tools and approaches can be tried out. | [5] |
| **Psychological testing for diplomats** | Diplomatic training sometimes incorporates psychological tests (e.g., Rorschach, TAT) to evaluate decision‑making and leadership potential, effectively “testing” a diplomat’s readiness for higher‑responsibility roles. | [7] |

### Putting it together

“**Test diplomacy**” in the supplied context refers to the practice of using **controlled, experimental or low‑stakes settings** to:

1. **Pilot specific agreements** (e.g., the 1963 Nuclear Test Ban Treaty) that act as early, limited tests of broader disarmament cooperation.  
2. **Iterate diplomatic processes** much like software in a beta phase—rules and norms are deliberately left incomplete so they can be refined as they are applied.  
3. **Experiment with new ideas** in security or internet‑governance forums where the pressure is reduced, allowing creativity and relationship‑building.  
4. **Assess and develop diplomatic talent** through psychological and scenario‑based testing before diplomats assume senior decision‑making roles.

Thus, “test diplomacy” is not a single treaty or event but a **methodological approach**: deliberately designing diplomatic activities as experiments that can be observed, evaluated, and improved.  

**Key citations:** nuclear‑test‑ban negotiations as early tests of bilateral diplomacy [1][2][3]; the “beta” analogy for ongoing diplomatic experimentation [4]; low‑pressure testing of ideas in security diplomacy [6]; workshop‑based testing at the IGF [5]; psychological testing of diplomats [7].

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**“Test diplomacy” — how diplomacy is treated as a laboratory or beta‑stage experiment**

The sources describe several ways in which diplomatic practice is deliberately **tested, iterated, and evaluated** before it is fully institutionalised:

| Aspect of “testing” | What the source says | How it shows diplomacy is being tested |
|-------------------|----------------------|----------------------------------------|
| **Beta‑style, experimental environment** | “Diplomacy today resembles a beta version: functional but still being tested, adapted, and iterated in real time… Rules are incomplete, institutions lag behind, and norms are tested while technologies are already deployed.”【4】 | Diplomacy is framed as a living prototype where concepts, rules and norms are trialled in real‑world settings. |
| **Workshops and low‑pressure pilots** | “This allows for testing new ideas and building personal relationships in a low‑pressure environment.”【6】 | Workshops act as test‑beds for novel diplomatic approaches and relationship‑building. |
| **Training‑related assessments** | “It would be reasonable to expect that these tests should be given again when the diplomat begins to make decisions, lead working teams, and hold executive positions…”【7】 | Diplomatic education incorporates formal tests (e.g., psychological inventories, scenario‑based assessments) to gauge readiness before real‑world decision‑making. |
| **Digital‑policy testing platforms** | “I was curious and looked at the IGF programme.” (IGF = Internet Governance Forum)【5】 | The IGF and similar forums serve as experimental venues for testing governance models for the internet. |
| **Purpose‑built testing sites** | “It was probably used for testing purposes.” (Diplomacy.edu website)【8】 | Dedicated online platforms are created expressly to trial diplomatic tools or curricula. |

### What “test diplomacy” means in practice
1. **Iterative norm‑making** – New diplomatic norms (e.g., around AI, cybersecurity) are introduced in pilot settings, observed, and refined before wider adoption.  
2. **Scenario‑based exercises** – Diplomats undergo simulated negotiations, psychological inventories, and role‑plays to test their decision‑making under controlled conditions.  
3. **Digital‑policy sandboxes** – Forums like the IGF act as sandboxes where internet‑governance proposals are trialled with multiple stakeholders.  
4. **Low‑stakes workshops** – Small‑group meetings allow participants to experiment with ideas without the pressure of formal treaty negotiations.  

Together, these examples illustrate that modern diplomacy is **explicitly treated as a testing ground**, much like a software beta, where ideas, norms, and personnel are evaluated, adjusted, and only then rolled out into the formal diplomatic arena.

</details>

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**Why sharing research and experiences matters**

- Practical experience “helps develop judgment on sharing research” and gives scientists the confidence to decide when and how to disclose new results [1][6].  
- Data sharing “promotes collaboration and knowledge exchange” across disciplines and borders [3].  
- Experiences from different regions and sectors “can be shared for mutual benefit” and help build a common understanding among stakeholders [4][8].  
- Both “documented knowledge” and “real‑world experience” are needed to convey a complete picture [2][7].

---

### A simple, context‑based roadmap for sharing research and experiences

| Step | What to do | Why it matters (context) | Tips from the sessions |
|------|------------|--------------------------|------------------------|
| **1. Clarify the purpose** | Define whether you aim for feedback, collaboration, policy influence, or capacity‑building. | Researchers weigh “the risk of being scooped against the benefits of feedback” [6]. | State the goal up‑front so participants know how to engage. |
| **2. Choose the right format** | • Formal paper or report  <br>• Presentation at a conference or forum  <br>• Short “experience‑share” post in a networking session | “Sharing information … not only by document, by real experience” [2]. | Mix formats: a concise slide deck for a live session and a detailed write‑up for later reference. |
| **3. Select an appropriate venue** | • High‑level sessions (e.g., IGF Open Forum, DPI High‑Level Session)  <br>• Specialised practitioner meetings (e.g., CSIRT networking session)  <br>• Regional workshops | “Different regions have various experiences that can be shared for mutual benefit” [8]. | Use venues that already bring together the relevant community; the IGF sessions repeatedly emphasized open sharing [4][7]. |
| **4. Prepare the content** | • Summarise the methodology and key findings  <br>• Highlight concrete lessons learned or “what worked / didn’t work”  <br>• Identify any sensitive data that must be protected | “If any of you heard something interesting … you can share as well” – encourages peer‑to‑peer exchange [4]. | Keep the narrative focused on actionable insights; avoid overly technical jargon when the audience is mixed. |
| **5. Manage risks** | • Assess intellectual‑property or security implications  <br>• Consider embargoes or partial disclosure if “risk of being scooped” is high [6] | Balancing openness with protection ensures long‑term credibility. | Offer a “preview” version for trusted collaborators while keeping the full dataset under controlled access. |
| **6. Invite feedback and dialogue** | • Allocate Q&A time in live sessions  <br>• Provide a contact point or online forum for follow‑up  <br>• Encourage participants to share their own experiences | “Sharing information … should not be only within certain layers but also between those layers to build understanding” [8]. | Use the IGF networking model: brief presentations followed by open comment rounds. |
| **7. Document the exchange** | • Publish a summary of the discussion (minutes, blog post, white paper)  <br>• Archive slides, recordings, and any contributed materials | “We show the fact or knowledge, experience, sharing those things is quite important” [2]. | Make the archive publicly accessible (e.g., on a community portal) to enable future reference. |
| **8. Reflect and iterate** | • Gather participants’ evaluations  <br>• Identify gaps for the next round of sharing | Continuous “practical experience … develops judgment” [1][6]. | Use the feedback to refine the next sharing session’s format or scope. |

---

### Quick checklist for a first‑time sharing event

- **Purpose statement** (e.g., “Seek peer feedback on early‑stage AI safety experiment”) – [6]  
- **Chosen venue** (e.g., IGF Open Forum #159) – [2][7]  
- **Materials prepared** (slides + one‑page experience summary) – [2]  
- **Risk assessment** (what can be disclosed safely) – [6]  
- **Engagement plan** (Q&A, follow‑up mailing list) – [4][8]  
- **Post‑event archive** (upload to community site) – [2]

---

### Bottom line

The sessions you referenced repeatedly stress that **effective sharing blends documented research with lived experience**, leverages **cross‑regional and cross‑layer platforms**, and **balances openness with prudent risk management**. By following the eight‑step roadmap above, you can turn your research and practical insights into a catalyst for collaboration, learning, and responsible innovation.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**Why sharing research and experiences matters**

- **Builds judgment and improves outcomes** – Maria Leptin notes that “practical experience in research helps scientists develop judgment on sharing their work” (e.g., deciding whether to present new results at a conference) [1][6].  
- **Creates collaboration and knowledge exchange** – Data‑sharing is explicitly described as a driver of collaboration and knowledge exchange [3].  
- **Enables mutual benefit across regions and layers** – Different regions have distinct experiences that, when shared, “facilitate knowledge sharing and collaboration among stakeholders” and “build understanding” across organisational layers [8].  
- **Moves beyond documents to lived practice** – Sharing should include “real experience, not only by document” [2]; participants are encouraged to “exchange comments and try to understand what was going to be discussed” [4].  
- **Supports inclusive, interdisciplinary development** – The IGF sessions link data governance, human‑rights, sustainable development and other interdisciplinary topics, showing that shared experiences can inform a broad set of policy areas [3][7].

**Practical steps for researchers and practitioners**

| Step | What to do | Why it matters (source) |
|------|------------|--------------------------|
| 1. **Document the context** | Capture not only results but the methodological choices, challenges, and “real‑world” conditions that shaped them. | Enables others to replicate or adapt the work; goes beyond “just a document” [2]. |
| 2. **Assess the risk‑benefit balance** | Weigh the chance of being “scooped” against the value of early feedback and community validation. | Helps develop personal judgment on when and where to share [1][6]. |
| 3. **Choose appropriate venues** | Use open forums (e.g., IGF sessions, CSIRT networking events) that bring together diverse stakeholders and regional perspectives. | Facilitates cross‑regional learning and multi‑layer interaction [4][8]. |
| 4. **Share in multiple formats** | Combine formal papers, data sets, and informal narratives (case studies, lessons‑learned talks). | Supports “sharing information … not only within certain layers but also between those layers” [8]. |
| 5. **Encourage two‑way dialogue** | Invite comments, questions, and counter‑experiences from peers; treat sharing as an exchange, not a one‑way broadcast. | “Exchange our comments and try to understand” fosters deeper collective insight [4]. |
| 6. **Link to broader policy themes** | Frame findings within sustainable development, human rights, privacy, or economic impact where relevant. | Aligns research with the interdisciplinary agenda highlighted in high‑level sessions [3][7]. |
| 7. **Maintain openness and responsibility** | Ensure data is shared responsibly (respecting privacy, security, and ethical constraints) while remaining as open as possible. | Reflects the “socially, economically, environmentally responsible” ethos of campus and IGF discussions [2]. |

**Key take‑aways**

- **Experience is a teacher**: Hands‑on work sharpens the ability to decide what, when, and how to share [1][6].  
- **Sharing is collaborative, not solitary**: It should happen across documents, conversations, and platforms, linking different regions, sectors, and governance layers [4][8].  
- **Balance risk with feedback**: Weigh potential loss of priority against the benefits of community critique and improvement [1][6].  
- **Embed sharing in broader goals**: Align research dissemination with sustainable development, human‑rights, and data‑governance objectives to maximize impact [3][7].

By following these principles, researchers and practitioners can turn their knowledge into collective value, fostering a more resilient, inclusive, and innovative digital ecosystem.

</details>

> **Judge:** Answer B covers more aspects (maintenance body, spec contents, implementation details), provides concrete names and examples, and cites sources more precisely, while both remain accurate.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

ECMAScript is the **standardized, general‑purpose programming language** that underlies JavaScript (originally from Netscape) and Microsoft’s JScript.  It is defined by the ECMA‑262 language specification and is a cross‑platform, vendor‑neutral language that forms an essential part of the Web‑browser application platform, while also being used in many other environments—including embedded and resource‑constrained devices [1][4][5][8].

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

ECMAScript is the **general‑purpose, cross‑platform, vendor‑neutral programming language** that underlies JavaScript and other implementations such as Microsoft’s JScript.  It is defined and maintained by **Ecma International** through a series of language specifications (e.g., ECMA‑262) and associated APIs.  The specification describes the language’s syntax and semantics, the execution environment, and the built‑in libraries that programs may use — and an implementation consists of the language plus its required and optional libraries 【1†L1-L4】【5†L1-L3】.  While originally created for the Web browser platform, ECMAScript is now used in many other environments, including embedded and resource‑constrained devices 【2†L1-L4】.

</details>

> **Judge:** Both answers comprehensively cover India's AI position with accurate, detailed points and proper source citations, making them equally strong.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

India is being framed as a **mid‑way, stabilising leader in global AI governance** – a democratic, data‑rich nation that can shape “democratic AI” rather than an “autocratic AI” model.

* **Strategic diplomatic stance** – India positions itself as a **stabilising force** that will not seek dominance but will help balance the competing models of AI regulation (the restrictive European approach, the laissez‑faire American model, and the state‑controlled Chinese system) [2][6].
* **Economic and technical credibility** – The country ranks **third globally in AI market competitiveness** (according to the Stanford AI Index) and is seen as an ideal host for the Global‑South perspective [3][5].
* **Democratic scale** – With **100 + million AI users** and the world’s largest democracy, India’s experience and scale make it pivotal in determining whether AI evolves as a democratic or autocratic technology [7].
* **Infrastructure advantage** – India can leverage its **successful digital public‑infrastructure** (e.g., UPI, digital identity) to build **sovereign AI** capabilities while maintaining independence from external tech dominance [1][4][6].
* **Global‑South representation** – By combining its market strength, democratic legitimacy, and data advantage, India is positioned to **represent the Global South** in AI policy discussions [5][8].

**In short:** India’s AI position is that of a **democratic, data‑rich, and economically competitive “middle path” leader**—leveraging its digital infrastructure and large user base to champion sovereign, trustworthy AI and to act as a stabilising diplomatic voice for the Global South in the worldwide AI governance arena. [1][2][3][4][5][7][8]

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

India is being positioned as a **major, democratic‑centric leader in the global AI ecosystem**.  The country’s strategy rests on four inter‑linked pillars:

1. **Leverage its digital public‑infrastructure** – India’s experience with UPI, digital identity and other large‑scale public platforms is being used as a model for responsible AI governance, while deliberately steering clear of the extremes of Europe’s highly‑prescriptive rules, the United States’ laissez‑faire approach, or China’s state‑controlled model【1†L1-L4】【6†L1-L4】.  

2. **Showcase market competitiveness** – According to the Stanford AI Index, India ranks **third worldwide in AI market competitiveness**, giving it credibility as a global AI hub and a natural host for the Global South perspective【3†L1-L4】【5†L1-L4】.  

3. **Pursue “sovereign AI”** – With a population of over a billion, India has a unique data advantage and is seeking technological independence through sovereign AI capabilities, reinforcing its strategic autonomy【4†L1-L4】.  

4. **Act as a stabilizing democratic force** – As the world’s largest democracy with **100 + million AI users**, India is seen as a decisive factor in whether the future of AI will be “democratic” or “autocratic,” echoing historic choices made during the spread of the printing press【7†L1-L4】.  Its role in the AI Impact Summit underscores this diplomatic positioning【8†L1-L4】.

Together, these points portray India’s AI position as **a third‑ranked, data‑rich, sovereign‑focused, democratic leader that aims to shape global AI governance without seeking dominance**【2†L1-L3】.

</details>
